They differ from them only by being more rigidly academic (systematic pedantry) and by their fanatical and superstitious belief in the miraculous effects of their own social science.

For these reasons, they violently oppose all political action by the working class. According to them, such action can only result from a blind disbelief in their new "gospel."

The Owenites in England (followers of Robert Owen) and the Fourierists in France (followers of Charles Fourier), for example, oppose the Chartists (a working-class political reform movement in England) and the Réformistes (social reformers in France) respectively.

## CHAPTER FOUR

**HOW COMMUNISTS WORK WITH OTHER OPPOSITION PARTIES**

Chapter Two explained how Communists relate to existing working-class parties, like the Chartists in England and the Agrarian Reformers (those wanting land reform) in America.

**Working for Today, Preparing for Tomorrow**

Communists fight to achieve the short-term goals and support the current needs of the working class. But in the present movement, they also represent and look after the long-term future of that movement.

Here’s how Communists work with different parties in various countries:

* **In France:** Communists team up with a party known as the Social-Democrats. They work together against the conservative (wanting to keep things as they are) and radical (wanting big changes, but still capitalist) sections of the bourgeoisie (the capitalist class). However, Communists reserve the right to critically examine ideas and false beliefs (illusions) passed down from the time of the great French Revolution.

* **In Switzerland:** They support the Radicals. But they don't forget that this party is made up of conflicting groups. Some are Democratic Socialists (like those in France), while others are radical bourgeois.

* **In Poland:** They support the party that insists a revolution in land ownership (an agrarian revolution) is the most important condition for national freedom. This was the party that encouraged the uprising in Cracow in 1846.

* **In Germany:** They fight alongside the bourgeoisie whenever the bourgeoisie acts in a revolutionary way against:
    * The absolute monarchy (rule by a king with total power).
    * The feudal squirearchy (landowning feudal nobles).
    * The **petty bourgeoisie** (small-scale business owners and shopkeepers).

**Preparing for the Next Fight in Germany**

But Communists in Germany never stop, not even for a moment, doing something crucial. They work to make the working class very clearly understand the deep and hostile conflict between the bourgeoisie and the proletariat (the working class).
Why? So that the German workers can immediately use the social and political conditions that the bourgeoisie *must* create under its rule as weapons against the bourgeoisie itself.
And so that, after the fall of the reactionary (backward-looking) classes in Germany, the fight against the bourgeoisie itself can begin right away.

**Germany: The Focus of Attention**

Communists pay special attention to Germany for these reasons:
* Germany is on the verge of a bourgeois revolution (a revolution led by the capitalist class).
* This revolution is bound to happen under more advanced conditions of European civilization than previous revolutions.
* Germany has a much more developed proletariat than England had in the 17th century or France in the 18th century.
* Therefore, the bourgeois revolution in Germany will be just the beginning (a prelude) of a workers' revolution that will follow immediately.

**Supporting Revolution Everywhere**

In short, Communists everywhere support every revolutionary movement that is against the existing social and political order.

In all these movements, they make the **property question** the most important issue, no matter how developed that question is at the time.

Finally, they work everywhere for the union and agreement of democratic parties in all countries.

**Our Aims Are Open**

Communists refuse to hide their views and aims. They openly declare that their goals can only be achieved by the forceful overthrow of all existing social conditions.
Let the ruling classes tremble at a Communist revolution.
The proletarians have nothing to lose but their chains. They have a world to win.

**Workers of All Countries, Unite!**

## Part II: Das Kapital (The People's Marx)

## Editor's Preface

The German revolution of November 1918 opened the era of socialism. "Socialism" and "socialization" are the watchwords of the moment. But what does socialism actually mean? Everyone, and not only the intellectuals, urgently needs to become familiar with its fundamental teachings.

The founder of scientific socialism was Karl Marx, who was born in Trier in 1818 and died in London in 1883. The fundamental teachings of socialism are set out in his principal work, *Capital*. Anyone who wants to understand the direction in which our society is now developing, and all the more anyone who hopes to take an active part in that development, therefore has an imperative duty to learn what that work contains.

That duty is far from easy to fulfill. Whoever sets out to read Marx's *Capital* meets an overwhelming number of difficulties. We can go further and say that, for the layman, the book is simply incomprehensible, and most people are laymen.

The first difficulty is the sheer size of the work: no fewer than 2,200 large printed pages, filling three volumes. Who can be expected to read all of that unless he is a specialist in political economy, especially if he has a profession to attend to? The second difficulty is Marx's way of expressing himself, which is unusually hard to follow. Flatterers, eager to praise everything a great man has done, have claimed that Marx's style is clear, precise, and easy to understand. That is not true even of the short essays he wrote for newspapers, and when the claim is made about his books on political economy, it is entirely false. To understand Marx, several conditions must be met. The reader must immerse himself deeply in the great thinker's work and reflect on it with his whole heart and mind; he must also submit to intense intellectual effort; and, beyond that, he must already possess a sound and thorough training in economics. The reasons for Marx's obscurity are not hard to see. He carried out an immense intellectual labor. He had mastered everything the science of political economy had taught before him, and through his own research he greatly enlarged the body of knowledge already gathered.

He examined every economic problem in minute detail and found entirely new solutions to precisely the most important of them. The substance of economic science absorbed so much of his attention and energy that he gave no thought to the form in which he expressed his ideas. So many ideas occupied him at once that he was indifferent to how he set them down. And he evidently did not realize that many things he knew well, and which seemed self-evident to him, were bound to be extremely hard to grasp for readers without his vast knowledge. This was all the more true because he had no intention of writing for amateurs. On the contrary, he meant to produce a strictly scientific work.

Whatever the reasons, it is certain that understanding Marx demands far more time and labor than an ordinary layman can be expected to devote to the task.

This brings us to the third and greatest difficulty. Marx's work is cast in a single mold from beginning to end. The various parts of his teaching are so closely bound together that no one part can be properly understood without knowledge of the others. The reader of the early chapters naturally cannot know what the later ones contain, and so he is bound to form a false impression of the master's teaching until he has worked his way through all three volumes of *Capital*.

This difficulty is made worse by the fact that Marx himself was unable to finish the work. Only the first volume, published in 1867, was fully completed and prepared for the press by him. The other two volumes were published after his death by his friend Friedrich Engels, the second in 1885 and the third, in two parts, in 1894. Neither volume was anywhere near ready for publication when Marx died, so Engels often had to include the rough drafts in which Marx had first put his ideas on paper. The result is countless repetitions. A reader who does not know this, and the amateur cannot possibly know it, is astonished to find the same idea in different words recurring ten, fifteen, or even more times, without understanding why. As a consequence, even professional scholars usually confine themselves to the first volume, and so they inevitably misunderstand the author. This is all the more true of laymen, such as the socialist working man who, even if he devotes nearly all his leisure to reading the first volume through to the end, carefully avoids the second and third.

For all these reasons, it has long been clear to me that there was an urgent need to make Marx's *Capital* accessible to the great many people who are eager to know what it contains but cannot make its study their life's work. It must be clearly understood that this is not a matter of "popularizing" Marx's teaching, in the sense of another writer setting it out in his own words so as to make it comprehensible. There are already enough expositions of that kind, and they are often incomplete besides, since their authors frequently knew only the first volume and thought it unnecessary to consult the others. In this book, Marx himself speaks. It is his own work, in his own words, that I am placing before the reader, arranged so that anyone willing to spend a little time and effort on it can understand it.

I set myself this task many years ago. The war, and the enforced leisure it brought me, gave me the time I needed. I considered myself qualified to undertake it because I have been studying *Capital* closely and carefully for some thirty years. More than twenty years ago, at the request of the Institut des Sciences Sociales in Brussels and in collaboration with my Belgian comrade Vanderrydt, I translated the second and third volumes into French.

A few words are in order about how I have tried to carry out this task. As I have said, my first concern was necessarily to keep Marx's own words wherever possible and to limit myself to omissions and rearrangements. I noted above that the difficulty of understanding Marx stems largely from the fact that grasping any one part of his work properly requires knowing all the others. It is hardly an exaggeration to say that to a novice bold enough to attempt them, the opening sections must read as though they were written in Chinese. This is because he cannot yet have any idea of the spirit in which the book was conceived, or of the author's way of thinking and reasoning. To acquire that idea, he needs to know certain important discussions in the third volume. It was therefore clear to me from the outset that I would have to rearrange the order of Marx's ideas completely, and with it the order of the treatments in which those ideas appear. A considerable part of the third volume had to be moved to the very beginning. Discussions that in the original are scattered across several chapters, sometimes far apart from one another, often had to be brought together; others, by contrast, had to be broken up. As a result, I frequently had to write connecting sentences. On the whole, however, I have kept strictly to the exact wording of the original.

This alone yielded many advantages. Anyone who takes the trouble to compare the present edition with the original will be surprised to see how many lines of thought that are otherwise extremely hard to follow have become clear simply by being rearranged.

The omissions will prove just as valuable. Plainly, only one version of each of the innumerable repetitions in the second and third volumes needed to be kept. Beyond that, however, I never intended to reproduce the entire work in every detail. On the contrary, I had to make a selection that would allow the reader to study all of Marx's fundamental ideas in the author's own words without being daunted or exhausted by the book's enormous length. Anyone can check at any time, by comparison, whether anything essential has been left out. To make such checking easier, I have indicated at the start of each chapter, and elsewhere where necessary, which parts of the original I have reproduced.

It is true that a fair number of passages in this edition depart from the wording of the original. This was unavoidable, since otherwise they would have remained incomprehensible.

In closing, I venture to hope that this edition will not only help readers understand Marx better, but will also awaken interest in economic science generally and deepen understanding of it, and in that way serve the cause of socialism.

I would be especially glad if this abridged edition were to encourage readers to study the original work.

Berlin-Lichterfelde, April 1919.

Julian Borchardt

## Chapter I: Commodities, Prices, Profits

Political economy is concerned with how humanity is supplied with the goods it needs in order to live. In modern capitalist states this supply takes place entirely through the buying and selling of commodities: people come to own the goods they need by purchasing them with the money that makes up their income. Income takes various forms, but these can be grouped into three main kinds. Every year capital brings the capitalist a profit, land brings the landowner a ground rent, and labour power, under normal conditions and so long as it can find employment, earns the worker a wage. Capital thus appears to the capitalist, land to the landowner, and labour power (or rather, labour itself) to the worker as the three distinct sources of their respective incomes: profit, ground rent, and wages. Each of these incomes seems like the fruit of a tree that never withers, a harvest gathered and consumed year after year. More precisely, there seem to be three such trees, yielding the annual income of three social classes: the capitalists, the landowners, and the workers. The wealth that makes up every kind of income thus appears to spring from three separate and independent sources: capital, ground rent, and labour.

But the size of each class's income is not the only factor that determines how people are supplied with goods. The price of those goods is equally decisive, and the question of what determines prices has therefore always been a subject of close inquiry among economists.

At first glance the question seems to present no difficulty. Take any industrial product: its price is set by the manufacturer, who adds to the cost of production whatever profit is customary in his line of business. Price therefore depends on two quantities, the cost of production and the profit.

The manufacturer counts as cost of production everything he spends in order to produce a given commodity. This includes, first, what he spends on raw materials and any auxiliary materials he needs, such as cotton or coal, as well as on machinery, tools, and buildings. Second, it includes ground rent, such as the rent owed on his premises. Third, it includes the wages paid for labour. The manufacturer's costs of production can therefore be divided into three categories:

1) the means of production (raw materials, auxiliary materials, machinery, tools, and buildings);

2) ground rent (which is reckoned as a cost even when the factory stands on the manufacturer's own land);

3) wages.

If we examine these three categories a little more closely, however, unexpected difficulties arise. Take wages first. Depending on whether wages are high or low, the total cost of production, and with it the price of the finished article, will be high or low. But what determines the level of wages? The supply of and demand for labour power, one might answer. The demand for labour power comes from the capitalist, who needs workers for his enterprise, so a large demand for labour power implies a large growth of capital. And what does capital consist of? Money and commodities. Or rather, since money is itself only a commodity, as we will show in detail later, capital consists simply of commodities. The more valuable these commodities are, the larger the capital, and the greater the demand for labour power, which in turn affects the level of wages and, as a further consequence, the price of the finished article. Now consider what determines the value, or price, of the commodities that make up capital. Their value is determined by the costs required to produce them. And among those costs of production we find wages! According to this theory, then, the level of wages is ultimately determined by the level of wages, and the price of commodities by the price of commodities!

Alternatively, it is said that wages are determined by the price of the food the worker needs. But that food consists of commodities too, and their price is partly determined by wages, so the error here is plain.

The second component of the manufacturer's costs of production, as we saw, is the means of production. No detailed analysis is needed to see that cotton, machinery, coal, and the like are also commodities, and that exactly the same holds true for them as for the commodities that make up the worker's food or the employer's capital.

The attempt to explain prices by reference to the costs of production has thus failed badly. It has merely ended by "determining" prices through prices themselves!

To the cost of production the manufacturer adds the customary profit. Here, it might seem, all difficulties disappear, since the percentage, or rate, of profit he must reckon is known to him as the figure customary in his particular branch of industry. Special circumstances may, of course, lead an individual manufacturer to calculate his profit at a somewhat higher or lower rate than the customary one. But on average the rate of profit is the same across all the enterprises in a given branch. Each branch of industry thus has a common average rate of profit.

Nor is that all. Competition brings the rates of profit in different branches into a certain balance with one another, and it could not be otherwise. As soon as unusually high profits are being made in a particular branch, capital leaves the less lucrative branches and is invested in the more lucrative one. New capital, which is constantly being formed and constantly seeking profitable investment, will likewise flow heavily toward such a branch. Production there must then rise considerably, and in order to sell the greatly increased quantity of goods, prices, and with them profits, must fall. The reverse happens when profits in a branch are unusually low. Capital withdraws from it as quickly as it can, production shrinks to the extent of that withdrawal, and this in turn tends to raise prices and profits.

Competition thus tends to establish an equilibrium among the rates of profit in all branches, and we are justified in speaking of a general average rate of profit which, if not identical everywhere, is at least roughly similar in every branch of production. This equality is admittedly less obvious than the equality of profits within a single branch, because overhead costs, the wear on machinery, the intensity with which that machinery is used, and so on can vary widely from one branch to another. To offset these differences, the gross profit, meaning the percentage the manufacturer actually adds on top of his costs of production, may be noticeably higher in one branch than in others, or noticeably lower. This obscures the real state of affairs. Nevertheless, once the various costs of production have been deducted, the different branches of industry are left with a net rate of profit that is approximately the same in every case.

Since a general average rate of profit exists, the amount of profit any given enterprise actually earns depends on the amount of capital invested in it. It is true, as noted earlier, that it makes some difference whether a factory produces guns or cotton stockings, since profits vary slightly according to the security of the investment, the ease or difficulty with which the product can be sold, and similar factors. But these differences are minor. Suppose the general average rate of profit is 10 per cent: a capital of £1,000,000 must then yield a profit ten times larger than that yielded by a capital of £100,000. We assume here, of course, that the business is properly run, and we set aside all the particular strokes of good or bad luck that may occur in the history of any individual enterprise.

We must also bear in mind that profits are earned not only by industrial enterprises, which produce commodities, but also by commercial enterprises, which merely act as intermediaries in the sale of those commodities from producer to consumer, and likewise by banks, carriers and forwarding agents, railways, and so on. In all these enterprises, again assuming efficient management, profits are determined by the amount of capital invested. Small wonder, then, that the people engaged in actually running such enterprises should be convinced that profits arise, as it were, spontaneously out of capital, and that capital produces them just as well-cultivated soil produces crops. Insofar as profits are not regarded as a natural property inherent in capital, they are seen as the result of the work done by the capitalist. And in fact, throughout this discussion we have always had to start from the assumption that the business is efficiently managed. Much depends on the manager's personal competence. If he proves incompetent, the profit of an individual enterprise can easily fall below the general average rate, whereas a capable manager may succeed in raising it above that average.

## Chapter II: Profit and Value in Circulation

But how can profit arise "spontaneously" from capital? To produce a given commodity, the capitalist needs a certain sum, say £5. This sum covers all the costs of production: raw and auxiliary materials, wages, and the wear on machinery, tools, buildings, and so on. He then sells the finished commodity for £5 10s. If we conclude that the finished commodity is genuinely worth £5 10s., we must also conclude that this added value, which appeared during production, came from nothing, since every value for which the capitalist paid his £5 existed before the commodity itself did. Human reason cannot accept that something is created out of nothing. Economists have therefore always held, and still hold today, that the value of a commodity does not increase during production. When production is finished, the capitalist possesses only an object of the same value he started with, which in our example is £5.

Where, then, do the extra 10 shillings come from when he sells the commodity? The mere fact that the commodity passes from the seller's hands into the buyer's cannot increase its value, for that too would amount to creating something out of nothing.

Two explanations are commonly offered to escape this difficulty. Some argue that the commodity really is worth more in the buyer's hands than in the seller's, because it satisfies a want the buyer has and the seller lacks. Others say that the commodity does not actually possess the value represented by the price paid, and that the buyer pays the surplus without receiving anything equivalent in return.

Let us examine both views. The French economist Condillac wrote in 1776, in an essay on *Commerce and Government*: "It is false that equal value is given and received in the exchange of commodities. The opposite is true. Each of the two parties always gives a smaller value for a greater one.... If equal values were always exchanged, neither party would make a profit. Yet both profit, or at least both ought to. Why? The value of things lies solely in their relation to our wants. What is more to one person is less to another, and the reverse.... We want to give away something useless to us in order to obtain something we need; we want to give less in order to get more."

This is a strange piece of arithmetic. When two people exchange goods, can each be giving the other more than he receives? That would mean that if I buy a coat from a tailor for £1, the coat is worth less than £1 while it remains in the tailor's shop, and its value rises to £1 only once I take possession of it. Nor does it help to fall back on the claim that the value of commodities lies solely in their relation to our wants. Setting aside the confusion between use-value and exchange-value, which we will come to later, even if the coat is worth more to the buyer than the money, the money is worth more to the seller than the coat.

If, on the other hand, we accept the theory that commodities in general are sold above their value, the consequences are stranger still. Suppose for the moment that, through some inexplicable privilege, the seller can sell his goods above their value: for £5 10s. when they are worth only £5, a premium of 10 percent. The seller thereby gains 10 percent in value. But having been a seller, he now becomes a buyer. He meets a third owner of commodities, who as seller enjoys the same privilege of charging 10 percent too much. Our man has gained 10 shillings as a seller and lost 10 shillings as a buyer. The whole process comes down to this: every owner of commodities sells his goods to every other owner at 10 percent above their value, which is exactly the same as if all of them had sold at their true value. Prices rise, but the real relation of the commodities' values to one another stays the same.

Suppose instead that the buyer enjoys the privilege of purchasing commodities below their value. Here we need not even point out that the buyer will later become a seller again, since he was a seller before he became a buyer. He has already lost 10 percent as a seller before gaining 10 percent as a buyer. Everything remains as it was.

Someone may object that this cancelling of an earlier loss by a later gain applies only to buyers who go on to become sellers, and that some people have nothing to sell. Those who consistently hold the illusion that added value comes from a nominal rise in prices, or from the seller's privilege of overcharging, do assume the existence of a class that only buys and never sells, a class that only consumes and never produces. But the money with which such a class keeps on buying must come from the owners of commodities themselves, without any exchange and free of charge, on the strength of some title, legal or otherwise. To sell this class commodities above their value is simply to recover, partially and by trickery, money that was handed over for nothing. In ancient times, for instance, the cities of Asia Minor paid Rome an annual tribute in money. Rome used this money to buy goods from them, and paid too much for them. The inhabitants of Asia thus cheated the Romans, winning back part of the tribute through trade. Yet in the end it was they who were swindled, for both before and after, their goods were paid for with their own money. This is no way to grow rich or to create surplus-value.

We do not deny, of course, that an individual owner of commodities can enrich himself by overcharging. Owner A may be shrewd enough to cheat his fellow traders B and C, who for all their efforts never manage to get even. A sells B wine worth £2 and receives in exchange corn worth £2 10s. A has turned his £2 into £2 10s., making more money out of less. But look at the matter more closely. Before the exchange, A had £2 worth of wine and B had £2 10s. worth of corn, a total value of £4 10s. After the exchange, the same total of £4 10s. remains. The value in circulation has not grown by a single farthing, the smallest coin of the realm. All that has changed is the share of that total held by A and by B. The same shift would have occurred if A, instead of disguising the transaction as an exchange, had simply stolen 10 shillings from B. Changing how commodities are distributed plainly cannot increase their total value, any more than a Jew could increase the quantity of precious metal in a country by selling an eighteenth-century copper coin for a gold one: the collector who pays the gold loses exactly what the dealer gains, and the nation is no richer in metal. The capitalist class of a country, taken as a whole, cannot profit by cheating itself.

However we twist and turn the matter, the result is the same. If commodities of equal value are exchanged, no surplus-value arises; and none arises if commodities of unequal value are exchanged. The circulation or exchange of commodities creates no value in itself.

The increase in value that appears after a commodity is sold cannot, in any case, be derived from circulation itself. Nor can it be explained by a gap between a commodity's price and its value. If price really does diverge from value, we must reduce the one to the other, setting aside the divergence as a purely accidental phenomenon so that incidental disturbances do not confuse us. This kind of reduction is not confined to science. The constant fluctuations of market prices, their rises and falls, cancel one another out and settle into an average price, which is their underlying governing principle. This average guides the merchant or the industrial entrepreneur in every long-term undertaking. The merchant knows that over a long enough period, taken as a whole, commodities are in fact sold at their average price, neither above it nor below it. The origin of profit, the creation of surplus-value, must therefore be explained on the assumption that commodities are sold at their real value. And in that case surplus-value must clearly originate in the process of production. At the very moment the commodity is finished, before it leaves the hands of its first seller, it must already be worth as much as the final buyer, the consumer, pays for it at the end. Its value must exceed what it cost the manufacturer to produce it; during its production, a new value must have been created.

This brings us to the question of how the value of commodities arises.

## Chapter III: Value in Use and Exchange-Value; the Socially Necessary Labour

A commodity is, first of all, an external object that satisfies some human want through its qualities. Every useful thing, such as iron or paper, can be considered from two points of view: quality and quantity. Each has many qualities and can therefore be useful in many ways. Its usefulness makes it a value in use. This usefulness does not float freely in the air. It depends on the physical qualities of the commodity and cannot exist apart from them. The material substance of the commodity, whether iron, wheat, or diamond, is therefore itself a value in use.

Exchange value appears, at first, as the quantitative ratio in which use values of one kind are traded for use values of another. A definite quantity of one commodity is regularly exchanged for a definite quantity of another, and that ratio is its exchange value. The ratio shifts constantly from one time and place to another, so exchange value seems accidental and purely relative. As the French philosopher Condillac put it, it seems "to consist solely in the relation of the commodities to our wants." On this view, a commodity could not possess an exchange value of its own. The question needs closer examination.

Take a single commodity, such as a ton of wheat. It trades for a certain amount of boot polish, or silk, or gold, and for other commodities in varying proportions. The wheat therefore has many different exchange values. But because each of those amounts of boot polish, silk, and gold represents the exchange value of a ton of wheat, they must all have equal exchange value with one another. Two conclusions follow. First, the various valid exchange values of the same commodity express something identical. Second, there must be something underlying exchange value, something of which exchange value is merely the form of expression.

Now take two commodities, such as wheat and iron. Whatever the ratio in which they exchange, it can always be written as an equation in which a given quantity of wheat equals a certain quantity of iron. Suppose, for example, that one ton of wheat equals two tons of iron. What does this equation mean? It means that a common property of the same magnitude exists in two different things, in the ton of wheat and in the two tons of iron. Both are therefore equal to a third thing, which is neither wheat nor iron. Insofar as each is an exchange value, each must be reducible to this third thing.

This common property cannot be any natural quality of the commodities. Their physical qualities matter only insofar as they make the commodities useful, that is, insofar as they give them value in use. In exchange, however, the use value of commodities is effectively set aside. One use value is worth exactly as much as any other, provided it is available in the right proportion. The economist Nicholas Barbon wrote in 1696: "One kind of commodity is as good as another, if their exchange values are equal. There is no difference, and no possibility of distinction, between things of equal exchange value. . . . Five pounds' worth of lead or iron has the same exchange value as five pounds' worth of silver and gold." When we regard commodities as use values, their defining feature is difference in quality. When we regard them as exchange values, they can differ only in quantity.

If we set aside the use value of commodities, only one property remains to them: they are products of labour. Yet the product of labour itself changes in our hands once we do this. By ignoring its use value, we also ignore the material components and forms that give it use value. It is no longer a table, a house, a length of yarn, or any useful object at all. All its concrete qualities have vanished from view. Nor is it any longer the product of a carpenter, a stonemason, a spinner, or any other particular kind of productive worker. It has become simply the product of human labour as such, abstract human labour: the expenditure of human labour power regardless of the form that expenditure took. Whether a carpenter, a stonemason, or a spinner did the work no longer matters. Seen from this angle, every product of labour shows only that human labour power was spent in making it and that labour has accumulated in it.

A commodity therefore has exchange value only because, and only to the extent that, abstract human labour is embodied in it. How is the magnitude of this value measured? By the quantity of the "value-creating substance," labour, that it contains. The quantity of labour is measured by its duration, and working time is measured in turn by definite units such as hours and days.

If the value of a commodity is determined by the amount of labour spent producing it, it might seem that the lazier and clumsier a worker is, the more valuable his product, since he needs more time to make it. But the labour that forms the substance of value is homogeneous human labour, the expenditure of one uniform human labour power. The total labour power of society, embodied in the sum of the values of all commodities in existence at a given moment, must be treated as a single homogeneous mass of human labour power, even though it is made up of the labour power of countless individuals. Each individual's labour power counts as equal to every other's insofar as it has the character of society's average labour power and functions as such, that is, insofar as it takes only the working time necessary on average, or socially necessary, to produce a commodity. Socially necessary working time is the time required to produce a use value under the normal conditions of production of the day, with the average degree of skill and intensity then prevailing. After steam-powered looms were introduced in England, for example, perhaps only half the labour previously needed was required to weave a given quantity of yarn into cloth. The English hand-weaver still needed the same time as before to do this, but an hour of his individual work now represented only half an hour of social labour, and its product consequently lost half its former value.

It is therefore only the quantity of labour, or the working time, socially necessary for its production that determines the exchange value of a commodity. Each individual commodity counts here merely as an average specimen of its kind. Commodities that contain equal amounts of labour, or that can be produced in the same working time, therefore have the same value. The value of one commodity stands to the value of any other in the same ratio as the working time needed to produce the one stands to the working time needed to produce the other. "As values, all commodities are only definite quantities of congealed working time."

The value of a commodity would therefore stay constant if the working time needed to produce it stayed constant. But that working time changes with every change in the productivity of labour. Productivity depends on many circumstances, among them the average skill of the workers, the level of scientific development and its practical application to technology, the way production is organized, the scale and efficiency of the means of production, and natural conditions. The same quantity of labour, for instance, yields twice as much wheat in a good season as in a bad one. The same quantity of labour extracts more metal from rich mines than from poor ones, and so on. Diamonds are rarely found on the surface of the earth, so finding them generally takes a great deal of working time. As a result, they represent a large quantity of labour in a small space. If mines were richer, the same quantity of labour would be embodied in more diamonds, and their value would fall. If carbon could be turned into diamonds with only a little labour, the value of diamonds might drop below that of bricks. In general, the greater the productivity of labour, the shorter the working time needed to produce an article, the smaller the mass of labour it contains, and the lower its value. Conversely, the lower the productivity of labour, the longer the working time needed to produce an article, and the higher its value.

A thing can have use value without having value. This happens when no labour is needed to make it useful to people, as with air, virgin soil, natural pasture, and wild timber. A thing can also be useful and the product of human labour without being a commodity. Someone who satisfies his own wants with the products of his own labour creates use values, but not commodities. To produce commodities, he must create not merely use values but use values for others, that is, social use values. Finally, nothing can have value unless it is an object of use. If a thing is useless, the labour contained in it is useless as well. It does not count as labour and therefore creates no value.

## Chapter IV: Purchase and Sale of Labour Power

We have now established that the value of commodities consists solely of the human labour they contain. We can therefore return to our earlier question: how can a manufacturer draw from his commodities a greater value than the value he put into them?

Let us state the case once more. A capitalist needs a definite sum, say £5, to produce a certain commodity. He then sells the finished product for £5 10s. Our investigation has shown that this surplus value of ten shillings cannot have arisen in the process of circulation, that is, in the buying and selling of commodities. It must therefore originate in the process of production, and our task now is to show how this happens.

Part of the problem is solved once we recognize that value is created by socially necessary labour. To produce yarn from the available means of production, the spindles and the cotton, labour is performed in the spinning mill. To the extent that this labour is socially necessary, it creates value. It adds new value to the means of production already present, in this case the raw cotton, while also transferring to the yarn the value of the machinery and other equipment worn out in the process. One difficulty remains, however. The capitalist appears to have already counted payment for this newly performed labour among his costs of production, since wages are reckoned among those costs alongside the value of the machines, buildings, raw materials, and other requirements. Those wages are paid precisely for the work of spinning. It therefore seems that all the value present after production was already present before it.

Yet the value newly created by the work of spinning need not be identical to the value the capitalist pays out in wages. It can be larger or smaller. If it is larger, we will have found the origin of surplus value.

But did we not begin from the assumption that every purchase and sale is made at exact value? Did we not satisfy ourselves that although price often deviates from value, such deviations explain nothing? For this reason, the case in which the capitalist pays the labourer less than the value of what he sells, however often it may occur, must be treated here as an exception. The origin of surplus value has to be explained in the normal case, in which the capitalist pays the full value of everything he buys for the purpose of production. The particular transaction of purchase and sale between capitalist and labourer therefore demands closer scrutiny.

What the capitalist obtains when he pays wages, and thus what he buys from the labourer, is the labourer's capacity, or power, to work. Several conditions must be met before the capitalist can buy this labour power. Labour power can appear on the market as a commodity only insofar as, and only because, its owner offers it for sale. To sell it as a commodity, the owner must be able to dispose of it freely; he must be a free person, the free proprietor of his own capacity to work. He and the capitalist meet in the market and deal with each other as owners of commodities with equal rights, differing only in that one is the buyer and the other the seller. They meet, in short, as legal equals. For this relationship to continue, the owner of labour power must sell it only for a specified period of time. If he were to sell it outright, once and for all, he would be selling himself, turning himself from a free man into a slave. He would cease to be an owner of commodities and would become a commodity himself.

The second essential condition for the capitalist to find labour power on the market as a commodity is that its owner cannot sell commodities in which his labour is embodied, and is instead compelled to offer his labour power itself, which exists only in his living body. This is the case when he owns none of the means of production needed to manufacture commodities, such as raw materials and tools, and has no stock of food to keep him alive until the work is finished and its products are sold.

The capitalist must therefore find in the market a labourer who is free in a double sense. He is free as a person, able to dispose of his labour power as his own commodity. And he is free in the sense of having no other commodity to sell: unattached to anything, and stripped of everything he would need to put his labour power to use on his own account.

Why this free labourer confronts him in the market is a question that does not interest the capitalist, and for the moment it does not interest us either. One thing, however, is clear. Nature does not produce capitalists and owners of commodities on one side and, on the other, people who own nothing but their own labour power. This arrangement is not natural, nor is it social in the sense of being common to every period of history. It is plainly the result of earlier historical development, the product of many economic revolutions and of the disappearance of a whole series of older forms of social production.

This peculiar commodity, labour power, must now be examined more closely. Like every other commodity, it has value. How is that value determined?

The value of labour power, like that of any other commodity, is determined by the working time necessary to produce it, and therefore also to reproduce it. Labour power exists only as a capacity of a living individual, and so it presupposes that individual's existence. A living person needs a certain quantity of necessities to sustain himself. The working time needed to produce labour power therefore comes down to the working time needed to produce those necessities. The value of labour power, then, is the value of the means of subsistence required to keep its owner alive.

These necessities must be sufficient to maintain the working individual in his normal condition of life. Natural needs such as food, clothing, heating, and housing vary with the climate and other natural conditions of each country. At the same time, the extent of these so-called natural needs, and the way they are satisfied, depend largely on the level of civilization a country has reached. They depend in particular, among other factors, on the conditions under which its class of free labourers was formed, and therefore on the customs and standard of living that class has come to expect. Unlike the case of any other commodity, the value of labour power thus contains a historical and a moral element. Nevertheless, for a given country at a given time, the average quantity of indispensable necessities is known in practice.

The owner of labour power is mortal. If such owners are to appear on the market continually, as the unceasing demands of capital require, then the labour power withdrawn from the market through wear and tear or death must be constantly replaced by at least an equal amount of new labour power. The total of necessities required to produce labour power therefore includes those needed by the replacements, that is, by the labourer's children. It also includes the costs of acquiring the skill and dexterity a particular branch of work requires, although for ordinary labour power these costs are negligible.

The value of labour power is the value of a definite quantity of the means of subsistence. It changes as the value of those necessities changes, which is to say, as the working time needed to produce them changes. Some necessities, such as food and fuel, are used up daily and must be replaced daily. Others, such as clothing and furniture, wear out more slowly and need replacing only at longer intervals. Some goods must be bought or paid for every day, others every week, every quarter, and so on. However these costs are spread across, say, a year, they must be covered by the labourer's average income, taking one day with another. The true daily value of labour power is therefore found by adding up the value of all the necessities the labourer requires over a full year and dividing that sum by 365. Suppose the commodities needed for an average day contain six hours of social labour. Then labour power represents half a day's average social labour each day; in other words, half a working day is required to produce a day's labour power. This quantity of labour, needed for the daily production of labour power, constitutes its daily value, or, if one prefers, the value of the labour power reproduced each day. If half a day's average social labour is also embodied in a quantity of gold worth three shillings, then three shillings is the price corresponding to the daily value of labour power. If the owner of labour power offers it for three shillings a day, its selling price equals its value, and we have assumed that the capitalist pays this value.

Because of the peculiar nature of the commodity we call labour power, its use value has not actually passed to the buyer at the moment the contract between buyer and seller is concluded. The use value of labour power consists in the later exercise of that power. The sale of labour power and its exercise are therefore separated in time. With commodities whose use value is sold at one moment and actually handed over to the buyer at a later one, payment is generally made afterward. In every country with capitalist production, labour power is paid for only after it has been exercised, at the end of the week, for example. Everywhere, then, the labourer advances the use value of his labour power to the capitalist; he allows the buyer to consume it before receiving its price. The labourer everywhere extends credit to the capitalist.

## Chapter V: How Surplus-Value Arises

Labor power, put to use, is labor itself. Whoever buys labor power consumes it by setting its seller to work. With a connoisseur's eye, the capitalist has chosen the means of production and the labor power best suited to his particular line of business, whether a spinning mill, a shoe factory, or some other enterprise, and he now has the worker consume those means of production through his labor. At first he must take labor power exactly as he finds it, which means accepting labor of the kind that existed before there were any capitalists. The changes in methods of production that follow from labor's subordination to capital can come only later, and so they must be examined later.

When we regard the labor process as the consumption of labor power that has been sold to a capitalist, it shows two distinctive features.

First, the worker labors under the capitalist's control. The capitalist makes sure that the work is done properly and that the means of production are used appropriately. During the labor process, the worker has no freedom and no independence.

Second, the product belongs to the capitalist, not to the worker. Since the capitalist, by our assumption, pays the daily value of the labor power, the right to use that power for the day is his. The other elements needed to make the product, namely the means of production, belong to him as well. The labor process therefore takes place among things the capitalist has bought, and so the product is his property.

This product is a use value: yarn, boots, and the like. Yet although boots are, in a certain sense, a foundation of social progress, our capitalist, a thoroughly progressive man, does not make them for their own sake. Use values are produced only because, and only to the extent that, they carry exchange value. Our capitalist has two aims. First, he wants to produce a use value that has an exchange value, an article meant for sale, a commodity. Second, he wants to produce a commodity worth more than the means of production and the labor power for which he laid out his money on the market. He wants to produce not merely a use value but value, and not merely value but surplus-value.

We know that the value of every commodity is determined by the quantity of labor it contains. This holds equally for the product that the labor process yields to our capitalist. Our first task, then, is to calculate how much labor has been embodied in it.

Take yarn as an example. To produce yarn, raw material is needed first: say, 10 pounds of cotton. There is no need at this point to ask how the cotton's value came about, since we assume the capitalist bought it at its value, for instance 10 shillings. In the price of the cotton, the labor required to produce it is already expressed as average social labor. Let us further assume that the instruments of labor worn out in processing the cotton, the spindles and so on, have a value of 2 shillings. If a quantity of gold equal to 12 shillings is the product of 24 working hours, or two working days, it follows at once that two working days are embodied in the yarn. The working time needed to produce the cotton is part of the working time needed to produce the yarn, because cotton is the yarn's raw material, and so that time is contained in the yarn. The same is true of the working time needed to make the spindles, since cotton cannot be spun without wearing them down. We assume, however, that only as much working time is spent as is indispensable under the prevailing social conditions of production. If only 1 pound of cotton is needed to spin 1 pound of yarn, then only 1 pound of cotton may be counted in making 1 pound of yarn. The same applies to the spindle. Should the capitalist take a fancy to golden spindles instead of iron ones, the value of the yarn still includes only the socially necessary labor, that is, the working time needed to produce iron spindles.

We come next to the question of how much value the spinner's own labor adds to the cotton. We assume that spinning is simple, unskilled labor, the average labor of a given state of society. We shall see later that even if we assumed otherwise, the answer would remain the same.

It is essential that the work of spinning consume no more time than is necessary under the given social conditions. If, under normal conditions of production, 1⅔ pounds of cotton are turned into 1⅔ pounds of yarn in one working hour, then a working day counts as a twelve-hour day only if 12 × 1⅔ pounds of cotton are turned into 12 × 1⅔ pounds of yarn in it. Only socially necessary working time counts as creating value.

That the labor consists of spinning, that its material is cotton and its product yarn, makes no difference whatever to the creation of value. If the worker were employed in a coal mine rather than a spinning mill, the object of his labor, coal, would be supplied by nature. Even so, a definite quantity of coal cut from the seam, say one hundredweight, represents a definite quantity of absorbed labor.

When the labor power was sold, we assumed that its daily value came to 3 shillings, and that these 3 shillings embodied six working hours, meaning that six hours of labor are needed to produce the average quantity of necessities the worker requires each day. If our spinner turns 1⅔ pounds of cotton into 1⅔ pounds of yarn in one working hour, then in six hours he turns 10 pounds of cotton into 10 pounds of yarn. In the course of spinning, therefore, the cotton absorbs six working hours. This working time is represented by a quantity of gold worth 3 shillings. Through spinning, then, the value of the cotton has been raised by 3 shillings.

Now consider the total value of the product, the 10 pounds of yarn. They embody two and a half working days: two contained in the cotton and the instruments of labor, and half a day absorbed during spinning. This same working time is represented by a quantity of gold worth 15 shillings. The price corresponding to the value of the 10 pounds of yarn is therefore 15 shillings, and the price of 1 pound of yarn is 1 shilling and 6 pence.

Our capitalist is taken aback. The value of the product equals the capital advanced. The value he laid out has brought no return and produced no surplus-value. The price of 10 pounds of yarn is 15 shillings, and 15 shillings were spent: 10 on cotton, 2 on the instruments of labor consumed, and 3 on labor power.

The capitalist may protest that he advanced his money intending to make more money from it. But the road to hell is paved with good intentions, and he might just as well have intended to make money without producing anything at all. He turns to threats. He will never be caught like this again. From now on he will buy finished commodities on the market rather than make them himself. But if all his fellow capitalists did the same, where would he find commodities on the market? And he cannot eat money. Next he turns sanctimonious. His sacrifice deserves recognition. He could have squandered his 15 shillings; instead he spent them productively and turned them into yarn. Precisely for that reason, however, he now has good yarn rather than a bad conscience. Besides, as the old saying goes, where there is nothing to be had, even the king loses his rights. However meritorious his self-denial, there is nothing left over to reward it, since the value of the commodity produced is exactly equal to the sum of the values that went into producing it. He should console himself with the thought that virtue is its own reward. Instead he grows insistent. The yarn is of no use to him; he made it to sell. Then let him sell it, or, better still, let him in future produce only for his own use. Now he bares his teeth. Could the worker produce commodities out of nothing, with only his own limbs? Did the capitalist not supply the materials with which alone the worker could work, and in which alone his labor could be embodied? Since most of society consists of people who own nothing, has he not done society an invaluable service with his means of production, his cotton and his spindles? Has he not done the worker himself such a service, supplying him with the necessities of life into the bargain? Is this service to count for nothing? But has the worker not, in turn, done him the service of transforming cotton and spindles into yarn? In any case, services are not the issue here. A service is nothing more than the useful effect of a use value, whether that use value is a commodity or labor. What concerns us here is exchange value alone. The capitalist paid the worker a value of 3 shillings. The worker returned exactly the same value, in the form of 3 shillings' worth of value added to the cotton. Value has been exchanged for value. Our friend, so proud of his purse a moment ago, suddenly adopts the humble posture of his own worker. Has he not worked too? Has he not done the work of supervising and directing the spinners? Does that labor not also create value? His own foreman and his manager shrug their shoulders. By now, though, he has recovered his former smile. He was having fun with us through that whole rigmarole, and he does not care a straw about any of it. He leaves these and similar hollow excuses and evasions to the professor of political economy, who is paid specifically to repeat them. The capitalist himself is a practical man who may not always weigh what he says outside his office, but who always knows exactly what he is doing inside it.

Let us look at the matter more closely. The daily value of the labor power was 3 shillings because half a day's labor is embodied in it, that is, because the necessities required each day to produce that labor power cost half a working day. But the past labor embodied in the labor power and the living labor it can set in motion are two very different magnitudes. That half a day's work is enough to keep the worker alive for twenty-four hours in no way prevents him from working the whole day. The value of labor power and the use of that power in the labor process are two distinct things. It was this difference in value that the capitalist had in mind when he bought the labor power. Its useful quality, its capacity to produce yarn or boots, was only a necessary secondary condition, since labor must be performed in some useful form if it is to create value. The decisive factor was the peculiar use value of this commodity: it is a source of value, and of more value than it possesses itself. This is the service the capitalist expected from it.

In acting this way, he obeyed the eternal laws that govern the exchange of commodities. The seller of labor power, like the seller of any other commodity, realizes its exchange value and parts with its use value. The use value of his labor power, the labor itself, no more belongs to its seller than the use value of oil already sold belongs to the oil merchant. The capitalist has paid the daily value of the labor power, so its use for the day, the whole day's labor, belongs to him. The daily upkeep of labor power costs only half a working day, although that labor power can work for the entire day, and so the value its use creates in one day is twice its own daily value. This circumstance is certainly a piece of great good fortune for the buyer, but it is in no sense an injustice to the seller.

Our capitalist foresaw this situation, and it was the source of his good humor. The worker therefore finds in the workshop the means of production needed not just for six hours of work but for twelve. If 10 pounds of cotton absorbed six working hours and were turned into 10 pounds of yarn, then 20 pounds of cotton will absorb twelve working hours and be turned into 20 pounds of yarn. Consider the product of this lengthened labor process. Five working days are now embodied in the 20 pounds of yarn: four in the cotton and in the steel worn away from the spindles, and one absorbed by the cotton during spinning. Expressed in gold, five working days are worth 30 shillings, so that is the price of the 20 pounds of yarn. A pound of yarn still costs 1 shilling and 6 pence. But the total value of the commodities that entered the process was 27 shillings, while the value of the yarn is 30 shillings. The value of the product exceeds the value advanced for its production by one-ninth. Twenty-seven shillings have become thirty. A surplus-value of 3 shillings has been obtained. The trick has worked at last.

Every condition of the problem has been met, and the laws of commodity exchange have not been violated in the slightest. Equal value was exchanged for equal value. As a buyer, the capitalist paid the full value of every commodity: cotton, spindles, and labor power. He then did what every other buyer of commodities does: he consumed their use value. The consumption of labor power yielded 20 pounds of yarn worth 30 shillings. The capitalist now returns to the market and sells commodities, having previously bought them. He sells the yarn at 1 shilling and 6 pence a pound, not a farthing above or below its value. And yet he draws 3 shillings more out of circulation than he originally put into it.

If we compare the process of creating value with the process of creating surplus-value, we find that the second is simply the first extended beyond a certain point. If the process continues only until the value that capital paid for labor power has been replaced by an exact equivalent, it is merely a process of producing value. If it continues past that point, it becomes a process of producing surplus-value.

Labor creates value, however, only to the extent that the time spent producing a use value is socially necessary. Labor power must be expended under normal conditions. If the self-acting mule, the automatic spinning machine, is the tool in general use for spinning, the worker must not be handed a distaff and spinning wheel, the old hand implements. Nor must he be given, in place of cotton of normal quality, trash liable to break at any moment. In either case he would spend more working time than is socially necessary to produce a pound of yarn, and that excess time would create neither value nor money. Labor power must itself be normal as well. In its branch of production it must possess the general average level of skill, dexterity, and speed. It must be expended with the general average degree of effort and with the intensity customary in society at the given time. The capitalist takes as much care to see that this is so as he does to see that his workers are never idle for a single moment. He has bought the labor power for a specific length of time, and he insists on his rights. He will not allow himself to be robbed. Nor may raw materials and tools be misused, because wasted raw materials or tools represent a useless expenditure of labor, and so they do not count in the product or enter into its value.

We have already noted that, for the production of surplus-value, it makes no difference whether the labor the capitalist buys is simple, unskilled labor of average quality or labor of a more complex kind. Higher, more complex labor is the expression of a labor power that cost more to develop, whose production therefore required more working time, and which consequently has a higher value than unskilled labor power. Because this labor power is of greater value, it is expended in labor of a higher grade, and in the same span of time it embodies itself in proportionately greater values than unskilled labor does. But whatever differences in skill separate the labor of a spinner from that of a jeweler, the portion of the jeweler's labor that merely replaces the value of his own labor power is in no way different in quality from the additional portion with which he creates surplus-value.

## Chapter VI: Constant Capital and Variable Capital; Fixed Capital and Circulating Capital

Now that we know that surplus-value arises during the production of commodities, and how it arises, it follows that the surplus-value obtained in each individual enterprise must vary in amount independently of the size of the capital. We have seen that surplus-value comes exclusively from living, newly performed labour, and not from the means of production that already existed before work began. Return to our example of the cotton spinner. The capitalist paid 24 shillings for all the means of production, the cotton and the instruments of labour, and 3 shillings in wages. The work of spinning did not change the value of those 24 shillings' worth of means of production; it simply passed exactly the same value on to the yarn. The 3 shillings paid in wages, by contrast, were consumed, and in their place we find a new value of 6 shillings.

The value of that part of the capital which the capitalist spends on means of production, that is, on raw and auxiliary materials and on instruments of labour, therefore does not change in the course of production. For this reason we call it constant capital.

The value of the part of capital spent on buying labour power, on the other hand, does change during production. It reproduces its own value and yields a surplus-value beyond it, and this surplus-value may be larger or smaller depending on circumstances. This part of the capital is continually being transformed from a constant, unchanging magnitude into a variable, changing one. We therefore call it variable capital.

Clearly, in different branches of production the means of production (constant capital) may stand in very different proportions to one and the same sum of wages (variable capital). In an engineering works, the quantity of instruments of production that labour power must put to use and transform will differ from the quantity in a cotton-spinning mill or a coal mine. The "organic composition" of capital, as we will call this ratio between its constant and variable parts, therefore differs from one branch to another. The widest range of ratios is not only conceivable but actually exists.

Suppose there are three different capitals, in three different branches, with the following organic compositions:

- I. 80c (constant) + 20v (variable)
- II. 50c + 50v
- III. 20c + 80v

If we assume that labour power is exploited to the same degree in all three branches, so that in each case it supplies exactly twice the value it receives in wages, we obtain the following result:

- capital I gains 20 shillings of surplus-value
- capital II gains 50 shillings of surplus-value
- capital III gains 80 shillings of surplus-value

Since profit is reckoned as a percentage of the entire capital consumed, this means profits of 20, 50, and 80 per cent respectively. We must also bear in mind that the exploitation of workers is not everywhere the same; it is greater in some enterprises and less in others. Other circumstances, too, help determine the amount of surplus-value in the various branches and even in individual enterprises, such as the speed with which the capital turns over, a subject we shall take up later. It follows that the amount of surplus-value actually produced cannot be the same in two different enterprises, still less in two different branches. How, then, despite all this, does the equal rate of profit that in fact prevails come about?

Let us take five branches of production, each with a different organic composition of the capital invested in it, and assume that in every case labour power supplies surplus-value equal to 100 per cent of its own value:

| Capital | Surplus-Value | Value of Product | Rate of Profit |
|---|---|---|---|
| I. 80c + 20v | 20 | 120 | 20% |
| II. 70c + 30v | 30 | 130 | 30% |
| III. 60c + 40v | 40 | 140 | 40% |
| IV. 85c + 15v | 15 | 115 | 15% |
| V. 95c + 5v | 5 | 105 | 5% |

Here we find very different rates of profit in different branches, even though the exploitation of labour remains the same throughout.

The total capital invested in the five branches comes to 500, the total surplus-value it produces to 110, and the total value of the commodities manufactured to 610. Now suppose that the figure of 500 represents a single capital, merely divided into the categories I to V. A cotton factory offers a comparable case: the ratio of variable to constant capital differs from one department to the next, from the carding room to the roving room to the spinning and weaving rooms, and the average ratio for the factory as a whole has to be calculated separately. On this assumption, the average composition of the capital of 500 is 390c + 110v, or, per hundred, 78c + 22v. If we regard each capital of 100 as representing simply one-fifth of the total, its composition would be this average of 78c + 22v, and every portion of 100 would likewise yield an average surplus-value of 22. The average rate of profit would therefore be 22 per cent, and the price of each fifth of the total product would be 122. The product of each fifth of the total capital advanced must therefore sell for 122.

To avoid false conclusions, we must take one further circumstance into account. Constant capital, the means of production, is itself made up of two essentially different parts. The means of production that make up constant capital are of various kinds. The chief ones are buildings, machinery, tools, and raw and auxiliary materials: on the one hand, the instruments with which labour is performed, and on the other, the materials to which labour is applied. Plainly, the first play an entirely different part in production from the second. The coal burned to heat the engine disappears completely; so does the oil used to grease the axle of a wheel. Dyes and other auxiliary materials likewise disappear, though they reappear in the qualities of the product. The raw material forms the substance of the product but changes its form. In short, raw and auxiliary materials are wholly consumed in the course of production, and nothing remains of the form they had when the process began. With the instruments of labour it is otherwise. A tool, a machine, a factory, or a vessel is useful only so long as it keeps its original form, only so long as it can be used tomorrow in the labour process in the same form it has today. And just as these things keep their own form, distinct from the product, throughout the labour process, they keep it even after they are worn out. Machines, tools, and factories always exist apart from the products they helped to make. If we consider the whole span during which such an instrument of labour remains in service, from the day it enters the workshop to the day it is consigned to the junk room, we find that over this period its use-value has been entirely consumed by labour, and its exchange-value has therefore been entirely transferred to the product. If a spinning machine wears out in ten years, for instance, its total value has passed, over those ten years of work, into the goods produced during that time. The working life of an instrument of labour thus spans a greater or smaller number of labour processes, repeated again and again. In this respect an instrument of labour resembles a human being. Every passing day brings a person twenty-four hours closer to death, yet no one can tell, merely by looking at a man, how much closer to that end he has already come. This does not stop life insurance companies from drawing very accurate, and very profitable, conclusions from the average length of human life. The same holds for instruments of labour. Experience tells us how long a given instrument, a particular type of machine, say, lasts on average. If we assume that its use-value in the labour process lasts only six days, then on average it loses one-sixth of its use-value each day, and so passes one-sixth of its value on to each day's product. The wear and tear on every instrument of labour is calculated in this way.

It is therefore evident that an instrument of production never transfers to the product more value than it loses itself through the using-up of its own use-value. If it had no value to lose, that is, if it were not itself a product of human labour, it would transfer no value to the product at all. It would help create use-value but would create no exchange-value. This is the case with all those means of production that exist independently of human labour: the soil, the wind, water, coal still lying in the seam, timber standing in virgin forest, and so on.

The instrument of labour must always take part in production with its whole physical capacity, even though the exchange-value it transfers is smaller. Suppose, for example, that a machine is worth 1,000 shillings and wears out in 1,000 days. In that case one-thousandth of the machine's value passes each day into the day's product. Yet the whole machine keeps working in the labour process, if with diminishing vigour.

What distinguishes this part of constant capital, the instrument of labour, is therefore that as soon as it goes to work and begins to wear, part of its value passes into the product while another part remains fixed in the instrument, and thus in the process of production. The value fixed in this way steadily diminishes until the instrument is worn out and has distributed its value across a quantity of products, the results of a long series of repeated labour processes. But as long as it continues to function as an instrument of labour, that is, as long as it need not be replaced by another of the same kind, constant capital remains fixed in it, while another part of the value originally fixed in it passes into the product and so circulates as a component of the commodity's value.

The part of capital fixed in the instruments of labour circulates just like any other. The entire value of the capital is in continual circulation, and in this sense all capital is circulating capital. But the part we have just examined circulates in a peculiar way. It does not circulate in its physical, useful form; only its value circulates, gradually and piecemeal, to the extent that it is transferred to the product as that product circulates as a commodity. Throughout its working life, part of its value always remains fixed within it, independent of the commodities it helps to produce. Because of this peculiarity, this part of constant capital takes the form of fixed capital. All the other components of the capital advanced, by contrast, constitute circulating capital.

The difference in the way the various parts of capital transfer their value to the product must also affect the amount of surplus-value each individual capital produces. This same peculiarity also tends to obscure the origin of surplus-value.

When the capitalist looks at the finished commodity, the difference between constant capital (means of production) and variable capital (wages) does not strike him. He knows, to be sure, that part of his production costs, part of the commodity's cost-price, went on instruments of production and part on wages. He also knows that if production is to continue, he must once again spend the money from the sale of the commodity in the same way, on instruments of production and on labour power. But none of this tells him anything about where value and surplus-value come from. On the contrary, all he sees is that the value of the means of production reappears in the cost-price of the commodity exactly as it was before production began, and that the same is true of wages. Appearances thus obscure the essential difference between constant and variable capital, and the surplus-value on hand at the end of production seems to derive equally from every part of the capital.

The difference between fixed and circulating capital, by contrast, is perfectly obvious. Suppose that the instruments of labour were originally worth 1,200 shillings, not counting raw materials worth 380 shillings and wages of 100 shillings. Suppose further that in the course of production 20 shillings' worth of the instruments of labour are worn away. The cost-price of the product will then be 20 shillings for wear and tear, plus 380 shillings for raw and auxiliary materials, plus 100 shillings for wages, a total of 500 shillings. Leaving surplus-value aside, the capitalist holds this value of 500 shillings in his hands in the form of the finished product. But the machines, factory buildings, and so on still exist besides, with a total value of 1,180 shillings. Their value can hardly be ignored, and so in the capitalist's mind the matter appears as follows: 20 shillings of the commodity's value arose from wear and tear on the instruments of labour (fixed capital), and 480 shillings from the consumption of raw materials and the payment of wages (circulating capital). He reasons that everything he puts into production as raw materials and wages comes back to him through a single round of production, while the sum invested in instruments of labour stays in the process longer and returns only bit by bit. That sum must therefore be built up again bit by bit, so that once the machines and the rest are completely worn out, the money to replace them will be on hand. The difference between fixed and circulating capital is, so to speak, hammered into the capitalist's head. But in this sense wages, too, are counted as circulating capital without a second thought. Like the outlay on raw materials, wages must be recovered from a single round of production and be available for buying fresh labour power. In this way appearances lead wages (variable capital) to be lumped together with raw materials (part of constant capital), and the two together are set against the instruments of labour (the other part of constant capital). To the superficial observer, buildings, machinery, and the like stand on one side as fixed capital, while raw and auxiliary materials and wages stand on the other as circulating capital. In this way the essential differences between wages on the one hand and the other parts of circulating capital on the other are entirely obscured.

## Chapter VII: How Uniform Profit Is Obtained

We can now return to the question of how the difference between fixed and circulating capital affects the rate of profit. In our earlier table, we assumed that the whole of the constant capital reappears at once in the value of the product, which is to say that all of it is circulating capital. This may occasionally be the case, but it is not the rule. We must therefore account for the fact that, in general, only part of the constant capital is consumed in production while the rest remains. Depending on whether this remaining part is large or small, the surplus-value actually produced by several capitals of equal size will naturally vary, even when all other conditions are identical.
