Consider the following figures. Throughout, we assume a rate of surplus-value of 100 percent, meaning that labor power produces, over and above its own value, an equal amount of surplus-value:

| Capital | Surplus-Value | Rate of Profit | Consumed Constant Capital | Value of Commodities | Cost-Price |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| I. 80c + 20v | 20 | 20% | 50 | 90 | 70 |
| II. 70c + 30v | 30 | 30% | 51 | 111 | 81 |
| III. 60c + 40v | 40 | 40% | 51 | 131 | 91 |
| IV. 85c + 15v | 15 | 15% | 40 | 70 | 55 |
| V. 95c + 5v | 5 | 5% | 10 | 20 | 15 |
| Total: 390c + 110v | 110 | 110% | | | |
| Average: 78c + 22v | 22 | 22% | | | |

If we treat capitals I through V as a single capital, we find that here too the combined composition of the five is 390c + 110v. The average composition therefore remains 78c + 22v, and the average surplus-value likewise remains unchanged at 22 percent. If this surplus-value were distributed evenly among capitals I through V, the commodities would have the following prices:

| Capital | Surplus-Value | Value of Commodities | Cost-Price of Commodities | Price of Commodities | Rate of Profit | Deviation of Price from Value |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| I. 80c + 20v | 20 | 90 | 70 | 92 | 22% | +2 |
| II. 70c + 30v | 30 | 111 | 81 | 103 | 22% | −8 |
| III. 60c + 40v | 40 | 131 | 91 | 113 | 22% | −18 |
| IV. 85c + 15v | 15 | 70 | 55 | 77 | 22% | +7 |
| V. 95c + 5v | 5 | 20 | 15 | 37 | 22% | +17 |

Taken together, the commodities are sold at +2, −8, +7, −18, and +17 relative to their value: 26 above value and 26 below.

The differences in price thus cancel one another out through the even distribution of the surplus-value, which amounts to adding the average profit of 22 percent on the capital advanced to the cost-price of each of the commodities I through V. Exactly as much as one portion of the commodities is sold above its value, another portion is sold below it. Only by selling at such prices can the rate of profit be the same, at 22 percent, for every category from I to V, despite the differing organic composition of the five capitals. The prices arrived at in this way are the prices of production. The price of production of a commodity is therefore equal to its cost-price plus the average profit.

When capitalists in the different branches sell their commodities, they recover exactly the capital values consumed in the process of production. Surplus-value, or profit, is another matter. Of this, the individual capitalist does not receive the amount actually produced in the making of his own commodities. He receives instead whatever share of the total surplus-value of the entire capitalist class falls to his capital according to the prevailing average rate of profit. Every capital advanced, whatever its composition, earns each year, per hundred, the same profit that the totality of capital earns per hundred in that year. Where profit is concerned, the various capitalists resemble shareholders in a joint-stock company whose dividends are distributed at a uniform percentage. Each capitalist's dividend differs only according to the size of the capital he has invested in the common enterprise, that is, according to the number of his shares. Consequently, if we consider all branches of production together, the total price of production of all commodities in society is equal to their total value.

This claim might seem to be contradicted by the fact that the commodities one capitalist uses as means of production, such as machines and raw materials, have as a rule been bought from another capitalist, and their price therefore already includes that capitalist's profit. The profit of one branch of industry, in other words, enters into the cost-price of another. But if we add together all the cost-prices of the entire country on one side and all the profits on the other, the calculation comes out exact. Take an example. Linen is needed to make linen coats, and linen in turn requires flax. A group of capitalists therefore takes up the production of flax and invests in it a capital of, say, 100 (for instance, £100,000). If the rate of profit is 10 percent, the linen manufacturers must buy this flax for 110 and then sell their linen to the tailors for 121. The total capital employed in these three branches thus amounts to:

- in the production of flax: 100
- in the production of linen: 110
- in the production of coats: 121
- total: 331

This sum must yield a profit of 33.1, which is realized when the coats are finally sold for 133.1. Of this profit, however, the coat manufacturers receive only 12.1. When they bought the linen, they had to pay its producers a surplus of 21 above the original 100; the linen producers in turn keep only 11 of this and pass the remaining 10 on to the producers of flax. Each of the capitals involved thus receives the share of profit that corresponds to its size.

Once a general rate of profit has been established, so that the average profit in every branch adjusts itself to the size of the capital invested, it is only by accident that the surplus-value actually produced in a given branch matches the profit contained in the selling price of its commodities. As a general rule, profit and surplus-value are two different magnitudes. How much surplus-value a particular branch produces matters directly only for the total average profit of all capitals. It affects individual branches of production and individual capitalists only indirectly: an increase of surplus-value in a particular branch raises the total surplus-value available, and with it the average profit. But this process unfolds, so to speak, behind the individual capitalist's back. He neither sees it nor understands it, and it does not interest him.

The gap between profit and surplus-value in the various branches of production completely conceals the origin and true nature of profit. It conceals them not only from the capitalist, who has an interest in deceiving himself, but from the laborer as well. In practical experience, profit and cost-price stand opposed to each other, and this alone is enough to confuse the capitalist about what value really is. He does not have in view the total quantity of labor required to produce the commodity, but only the part of it he has paid for in the form of means of production, whether dead or living. Profit therefore appears to him as something separate from the inner value of the commodity. He is confirmed and hardened in this mistaken view because, in fact, the profit added to the cost-price in any single branch of production, which is naturally the only thing the capitalist has in view, is not determined by the formation of value taking place within that branch. It is fixed from outside, quite independently of it.

In practical experience, every portion of capital yields a uniform profit. Whatever the composition of a capital, whether it sets in motion three-quarters dead labor and one-quarter living labor or one-quarter dead and three-quarters living, and whether in the one case it absorbs three times as much surplus-labor and produces three times as much surplus-value as in the other, it yields the same profit in both cases. This assumes an equal degree of exploitation of labor, and it sets aside individual differences, which disappear in any event, since in each case we are looking only at the average composition of the whole branch. The individual capitalist, whose horizon is limited, is correct in believing that his profit does not come solely from the labor he himself employs or that is employed in his branch of industry. Where his average profit is concerned, this is quite true. But he has no idea to what extent this profit is determined by the total exploitation of labor by the total capital, that is, by all his fellow capitalists together. He is all the more ignorant of it because the bourgeois theorists themselves, the professors of political economy, have so far failed to reveal it. Economizing on labor, both on the labor needed to produce a given commodity and on the number of workers employed, and making greater use of dead labor, meaning constant capital, appear to be economically sound measures. How, then, could living labor be the only source of profit, when reducing the quantity of labor needed for production appears, under certain circumstances, to be the primary source of increased profit, at least for the individual capitalist?

## Chapter VIII: Methods by Which Surplus-Value Is Increased

Surplus-value is produced through the employment of labor power. Capital buys labor power and pays wages for it. Through his work, the laborer creates new value that belongs not to him but to the capitalist. He must work for a certain length of time simply to reproduce a value equivalent to his wages. Yet once he has returned this equivalent, he does not stop working; he continues for several more hours. The new value he produces during this extra time, value that exceeds his wage, constitutes surplus-value.

Capital therefore extracts surplus-value, in the first instance, simply by extending the working day beyond the "necessary" working time, "necessary" only in the sense that it is the time required to reproduce the wages paid for labor power. At first, capital subordinates labor under whatever technical conditions it happens to find in place historically. It does not, therefore, immediately change the methods of production. The creation of surplus-value through the simple lengthening of the working day was no less active in an old-fashioned bakery than it is in a modern cotton-spinning mill.

The working day, however, has an upper limit. It cannot be extended beyond a certain point, and that limit is set by two factors. The first is the physical capacity of labor power. Within twenty-four hours, a human being can expend only a definite quantity of vital energy, just as a horse can work, on average, only eight hours a day. For part of the day that energy must be restored through rest and sleep; for another part, a person must eat, wash, dress, and attend to similar needs. Beyond these purely physical limits, the lengthening of the working day also runs up against moral ones. The laborer needs time to satisfy his intellectual and social wants, whose extent and number depend on the general level of a society's development. Both the physical and the social limits are highly elastic, however, and leave enormous room for variation. And so we find working days of eight, ten, twelve, fourteen, sixteen, and eighteen hours, days of every possible length.

Capital's constant efforts to lengthen the working day provoked the resistance of the working class. In England, the country where capitalist production first took hold, this resistance led to bitter social and political struggles that lasted for centuries.

There are, however, other ways of increasing surplus-value. The first is to use labor power more intensively, so as to extract more from it within a given period of time. The second is to push the laborer's wages below the value of his labor power. Although this second method plays an important part in actual practice, we cannot consider it here, because we are assuming that all commodities, labor power included, are bought and sold at their full value.

That leaves, finally, the increase of what is called "relative" surplus-value. Here the situation is as follows.

Suppose the working day lasts ten hours, six of which are spent replacing the value of labor power. A definite quantity of surplus-value is then produced during the remaining four hours. If the working day can be extended to eleven hours, or if the output of labor within the ten hours can be raised, or if both measures can be combined, the amount of surplus-value will grow accordingly. What is gained in this way is an absolute increase in surplus-value.

If, on the other hand, the working day cannot be extended beyond ten hours, and production cannot be intensified, it may still be possible to shorten the "necessary" working time. We assumed that this portion of the day lasted six hours because that was the time needed to produce the necessities of life required to sustain labor power. If those necessities can be produced in less time and with less labor, five hours might suffice instead of six. In a ten-hour working day, five hours rather than four would then be available for producing surplus-value. In this case, surplus-value would have increased "relatively," that is, in relation to the working day.

To achieve such a "relative" increase in surplus-value, the commodities that laborers consume must be produced in less time. The productive power of labor must rise, so that a smaller quantity of labor yields the same quantity of goods. For this purpose, it is by no means enough for capital to take over the labor process in the form it has inherited from history and then simply make that process last longer. The technical and social conditions of the process, and with them the method of production itself, must be thoroughly transformed before the productivity of labor can increase. Only by this means can the value of labor power be made to fall, and the portion of the working day needed to reproduce that value be shortened.

For the value of labor power to fall, the gains in productivity must take hold in those branches of industry whose products determine the value of labor power: industries that produce the customary means of subsistence, or goods capable of replacing them. These include not only the industries that make the means of subsistence directly, but also those that supply them with their means of production. The value of a pair of boots, for example, depends not only on the shoemaker's labor but also on the value of the leather, wax, thread, and other materials. By contrast, a rise in productivity in industries that supply neither the necessities of life nor the means of producing them leaves the value of labor power untouched.

When an individual capitalist makes shirts cheaper, say, by raising the productivity of labor, he is not necessarily aiming to reduce the value of labor power. Yet only to the extent that he ultimately contributes to that result does he help raise the general rate of surplus-value. Built into capital, therefore, is an inherent and constant tendency to raise the productivity of labor, so as to cheapen commodities and, by cheapening commodities, to cheapen the laborer himself.

Because one and the same process both cheapens commodities and increases the surplus-value they contain, we arrive at the answer to a riddle: why does the capitalist, whose sole concern is the production of exchange-value, continually strive to lower the exchange-value of commodities? Within the limits of capitalist production, every development in the productivity of labor has a single aim: to shorten the part of the working day in which the laborer must work for his own benefit, and, through that very shortening, to lengthen the other part of the day, in which he works for the capitalist for nothing.

## Chapter IX: How Capital Revolutionises the Mode of Production

### (A) Cooperation

Capitalist production begins when each individual capital employs a comparatively large number of workers at the same time. Many workers laboring together, at once and in one place (or, if you prefer, in the same field of labor), to produce the same kind of commodity: this is both the historical and the logical starting point of capitalist production. In its methods of production, early manufacture, for example, can hardly be told apart from the handicraft trades of the guilds, except by the larger number of workers employed at once by a single capital. The master craftsman's workshop has simply been enlarged.

At first, then, the difference is purely one of quantity. Yet within certain limits a real change takes place. In every industry, each individual worker, whether Peter or Paul, differs to some degree from the average worker. Whenever a certain number of workers are employed together, these individual differences offset one another and disappear. Edmund Burke, drawing on his practical experience as a farmer in the eighteenth century, claimed that even among as few as five farm laborers working together, individual differences vanish, so that any five adult farm laborers taken together will do as much work in the same time as any other five. Whether or not he was right, it is clear that the combined working day of a large number of workers employed at once amounts to one day of average social labor. If a capitalist employs 12 workers for 12 hours each, he has at his disposal a working day of 144 hours. The labor of each of the twelve may depart more or less from the social average, and each may need a different amount of time for the same task, yet the capitalist counts each man's working day as one-twelfth of the total of 144 hours. If, however, the same 12 men were employed in six pairs by six different small masters, it would be a matter of chance whether each master produced the same value and so realized the general rate of surplus value. In individual cases there would be deviations. If one worker needed considerably more time to produce a commodity than is socially necessary, his labor would not count as average labor. Of the six small masters, one would therefore squeeze out more than the average rate of surplus value and another less. Across society as a whole these inequalities would cancel out, but not for the individual masters.

Even when the method of work stays the same, employing many workers at once transforms the material conditions of the labor process. Buildings in which many people work, storehouses for raw materials, containers, implements, utensils, and other things that are used by many workers, whether at once or in turn, are now consumed in common. Using these means of production more intensively does not raise their exchange value; they cost no more than before. This advantage grows in proportion to the size of the capital. A room in which 20 weavers work at 20 looms must be larger than the room of a single independent weaver with two apprentices. But it costs less to build one workshop for twenty people than ten workshops for two people each. The value of means of production concentrated for common use on a large scale therefore does not rise in direct proportion to their expansion or to the greater useful effect they yield. Used in common, they transfer a smaller part of their value to each product, and so the total value of the commodity falls. This saving in the use of the means of production arises entirely from their being consumed in common by a large number of workers, even when those workers merely labor side by side and do not assist one another.

When many workers labor together according to a plan, either side by side in a single process of production or in different but connected processes, they are said to work in cooperation.

The attacking power of a squadron of cavalry, or the defensive power of an infantry regiment, is essentially different from the sum of the attacking or defensive powers of its individual soldiers. In the same way, the sum of the mechanical forces exerted by isolated workers differs from the social force that develops when many hands take part at once in a single operation, such as raising a heavy weight, turning a winch, or clearing an obstacle. In such cases, isolated individual labor either could not produce the effect of combined labor at all, or could produce it only at a great cost in time, or only on a much smaller scale. What we have here is not merely an increase in the productive power of the individual through cooperation, but the creation of a new power: the collective power of masses. As John Bellers wrote in London in 1696, "As one man cannot, and ten men must strain to lift a ton of weight, yet one hundred men can do it only by the strength of a finger of each of them."

Beyond the new power that arises when many forces merge into one collective force, mere social contact, for most kinds of productive workers, produces a spirit of rivalry and a quickening of the animal spirits, the vital energies of body and mind, which raises the efficiency of each individual. A dozen people working together will therefore produce far more in their combined working day of 144 hours than twelve isolated men working 12 hours each, or than one man working twelve days in succession. The reason is that man, if not a political animal, as Aristotle maintained, is at any rate a social one.

Several men may be occupied together at the same time on the same work, or on the same kind of work, and yet the labor of each, as a part of the collective labor, may correspond to a distinct phase of the labor process. Because of cooperation, the object they are working on passes through all these phases more quickly. Suppose, for instance, that 12 masons form a line to pass stones from the foot of a ladder to its top. Each of them does the same thing, yet their separate acts form connected parts of a single operation, and the 24 hands of the line carry the stones up faster than they would travel if each man climbed up and down the ladder separately with his load. The object covers the same distance in less time. Labor is likewise combined whenever a building is taken in hand on several sides at once, even though here, too, the cooperating masons are doing the same kind of work. In their combined working day of 144 hours, the 12 masons make far more progress than one mason could in 12 days, or 144 hours. The reason is that a body of men working in concert has hands and eyes both in front and behind, and is to some degree present everywhere at once. The various parts of the work advance simultaneously.

If the work is complex, the mere number of people cooperating allows the various operations to be assigned to different hands and so carried on at the same time. This shortens the time needed to complete the whole. Destutt de Tracy made the point in his *Treatise on the Will and Its Effects*, published in Paris in 1826: "All together obtain a result which no single individual could obtain. One rows while another steers and a third casts the net or harpoons the fish; in this way the fishermen achieve a success that would have been impossible without cooperation."

In many branches of production there are critical periods, fixed by the nature of the work itself, within which certain definite results must be achieved. If a flock of sheep is to be sheared, or a field of wheat mowed and harvested, the quantity and quality of the product depend on the work being started and finished within a certain time. Here the duration of the labor process is prescribed in advance, just as it is in herring fishing, where the catch must be taken while the shoals are running. Finishing the task within the proper time depends on many combined working days being applied at once. The amount of useful effect depends on the number of workers, but that number is always smaller than the number of isolated workers who would be needed to do the same amount of work in the same period. It is for lack of such cooperation that large quantities of grain go to waste every year in the western United States, and large quantities of cotton in those parts of the East Indies where English rule has destroyed the ancient village communities. On the one hand, cooperation allows work to be carried on over a wide area, and it is therefore indispensable in certain undertakings, such as draining land, building dikes, constructing irrigation works, and laying out canals, roads, and railways. On the other hand, while it extends the scale of production, it also makes possible a relative contraction of the space in which production takes place. This narrowing of the field of labor, even as its scale expands, saves a number of expenses, and it results from the gathering of workers together and the concentration of the means of production.

Compared with an equal number of isolated working days, the combined working day produces a greater quantity of use values and so reduces the working time needed to produce a given useful effect. As our analysis has shown, this increase in productive power is in every case due to cooperation itself. But wage workers cannot cooperate unless the same capital, the same capitalist, employs them at the same time, and therefore buys their labor power at the same time. The total value of these labor powers, the sum of the workers' wages for a day or a week as the case may be, must already be in the capitalist's pocket before the workers themselves are brought together in the process of production. Paying 300 workers at once, even for a single day, requires a larger outlay of capital than paying a smaller number week by week over the course of a whole year. The number of workers who cooperate, the so-called "scale" of cooperation, therefore depends first of all on the amount of capital that the individual capitalist can advance for the purchase of labor power.

What is true of variable capital is true of constant capital as well. The capitalist who employs 300 workers, for example, spends thirty times as much on raw materials as each of thirty capitalists who employ ten men apiece. It is true that the value and quantity of the instruments of labor used in common do not increase at the same rate as the number of workers, but they do increase considerably. The concentration of large quantities of the means of production in the hands of individual capitalists is thus a material condition for the cooperation of wage workers, and the extent of that cooperation, the "scale" of production, depends on the extent of this concentration.

We saw earlier that, at first, the subjection of labor to capital was merely a formal result of the fact that the worker, instead of working for himself, works for the capitalist and therefore under him. With the cooperation of many wage workers, the rule of capital becomes a requirement for carrying on the labor process itself, a real condition of production. That a capitalist should command on the field of production is now as indispensable as that a general should command on the field of battle.

All combined labor on a large scale requires, to a greater or lesser degree, a directing authority that keeps the individual activities working in harmony and performs the general functions that arise from the movement of the whole productive body, as distinct from the movements of its separate organs. A solo violinist is his own conductor; an orchestra needs a separate one. From the moment the labor under capital's control becomes cooperative, this work of directing, supervising, and coordinating becomes a function of capital. And once it is a function of capital, it takes on special characteristics.

The driving motive, the whole aim of capitalist production, is first of all to extract the greatest possible amount of surplus value, and therefore to exploit labor power as fully as possible in the capitalist's interest. As the number of workers employed together grows, so does their resistance, and with it the need for capital to overcome that resistance by counter-pressure. Moreover, as the mass of the means of production grows, means that no longer belong to the wage worker, so does the need to control how they are used. The cooperation of wage workers is, further, brought about entirely by the capital that employs them at the same time. The connection among their individual functions, and their union into a single productive body, lie outside them; these are the work of the capital that brings them together and keeps them together. The link between their various labors therefore confronts them, in thought, as the capitalist's plan, and in practice as the capitalist's authority, as the will of another who subordinates their activity to his own purposes. The authority of the capitalist is therefore despotic. As cooperation grows in scale, this despotism develops forms of its own. The capitalist hands over the direct and constant supervision of individual workers and groups of workers to a special kind of wage worker. An industrial army of workers under the command of a single capitalist requires, like a real army, officers (directors and managers) and sergeants (foremen and overseers), who give orders in the capitalist's name while the work is being done.

The subjection and supervision of labor by capital thus have two distinct origins. The first is that all labor performed in common requires a directing authority. The second is that, in the capitalist era, such labor exists to produce surplus value for capital. These two origins must be clearly distinguished and must never be confused if we are to understand the true nature of the process.

We have seen that new productive forces arise from the mere fact that many workers labor in common, and that cooperation also heightens the forces already in existence. These advantages originate in cooperation. But cooperation begins only with the labor process itself, and once the workers have begun to cooperate, they have ceased to belong to themselves and have been absorbed into capital. The productive power the worker develops when working in cooperation is therefore the productive power of capital. This power develops free of charge whenever workers are placed under such conditions, and it is capital that places them there. The social productive power of labor, meaning the productive power that arises from cooperation with other workers, costs capital nothing, and the worker does not develop it until his labor already belongs to capital. For both reasons it appears as a power with which capital is endowed by nature, a productive power inherent in capital itself.

The colossal effects of simple cooperation can be seen in the gigantic works of the ancient peoples of Asia, of the Egyptians, the Etruscans, and others. As Richard Jones put it in his *Text-book of Lectures* of 1852: "It has happened in times past that these Oriental States, after meeting the expenses of their civil and military establishments, have found themselves in possession of a surplus which they could apply to works of magnificence or utility. In constructing these works, their command over the hands and arms of almost the entire non-agricultural population, together with the exclusive control of the monarch and the priesthood over that surplus, provided the means of raising the mighty monuments that filled the land. . . . In moving the colossal statues and vast masses whose transport still excites wonder, human labor was spent almost alone, and spent lavishly. . . . The number of the laborers and the concentration of their efforts sufficed. . . . The non-agricultural laborers of an Asiatic monarchy have little to bring to the task but their individual bodily exertions, but their number is their strength, and the power of directing these masses gave rise to those gigantic structures. It is the confinement of the revenues that feed them to one or a few hands that makes such undertakings possible." In modern society, this power of the Asiatic and Egyptian kings, the Etruscan priest-rulers, and their like has passed to the capitalist.

Cooperation of the kind found at the dawn of human development, among hunting tribes, for example, or in the agriculture of the Indian village communities, rests on two foundations. The first is common ownership of the means of production. The second is that the individual in these societies has no more torn himself free from the umbilical cord of his tribe or community than a single bee has freed itself from the hive. Capitalist cooperation differs from such cooperation on both counts. The occasional use of cooperation on a large scale in the ancient world, in the Middle Ages, and in modern colonies rests on relations of domination and servitude, chiefly on slavery. The capitalist form, by contrast, presupposes from beginning to end the free wage worker who sells his labor power to capital. Historically, however, this form develops in opposition to peasant farming and to independent handicraft production. Seen from their standpoint, capitalist cooperation does not appear as one particular historical form of cooperation among others; rather, cooperation itself appears as a historical form peculiar to the capitalist process of production, and as the feature that specifically distinguishes it.

The simultaneous employment of a large number of wage workers in one and the same process is the starting point of capitalist production. It is the first change that the real process of labor undergoes through its subjection to capital.

### (B) Division of Labour and Manufacture

Cooperation based on the division of labor takes its classic form in manufacture, and manufacture is the prevailing, characteristic form of the capitalist process of production from roughly the middle of the sixteenth century to the last third of the eighteenth. Manufacture arises in two ways:

(1) By assembling in one workshop, under the control of a single capitalist, workers from various independent handicrafts through whose hands a given article must pass before it is finished. A coach, for example, was formerly the product of a great many independent craftsmen: wheelwrights, harness makers, tailors, locksmiths, upholsterers, turners, fringe makers, glaziers, painters, polishers, gilders, and others. In coach manufacture, all these craftsmen are brought together in one building, where they work into one another's hands at the same time. It is true that a coach cannot be gilded before it has been built. But if many coaches are being made at once, some can be with the gilders while others are passing through an earlier stage. So far we are still in the domain of simple cooperation, which finds its materials ready to hand in the form of men and things. Very soon, however, an important change takes place. The tailor, the locksmith, the upholsterer, and the rest, now occupied exclusively with making coaches, each gradually lose through lack of practice the ability to carry on their old trade in its full range. At the same time, each man's activity, now confined to a single groove, takes on the form best suited to its narrowed sphere. At first, coach manufacture appeared as a combination of independent handicrafts. Gradually it becomes the splitting up of coach making into its separate detail processes, each of which hardens into the exclusive function of a particular worker, while the manufacture as a whole is carried on by all of them together. In the same way, cloth manufacture and a whole series of other manufactures arose from the combination of different handicrafts under the control of one and the same capitalist.

Second, manufacture can also arise in exactly the opposite way. A single capitalist employs, at the same time and in the same workshop, a number of craftsmen who all do the same work or the same kind of work, such as making paper, type, or needles. This is cooperation in its most elementary form. Each craftsman, perhaps with the help of one or two apprentices, makes the entire commodity, and so he performs in turn every operation its production requires. He still works in his old handicraft manner. Before long, however, outside circumstances lead to a different use of the fact that the workers are gathered in one place and are working at the same time. Suppose, for instance, that a larger quantity of the finished article must be delivered within a given period. The work is then redistributed. Instead of each man performing all the different operations one after another, each operation is now assigned to a different craftsman, so that all of them are carried out simultaneously. This chance redistribution is repeated, reveals advantages of its own, and gradually hardens into a permanent and systematic division of labour. The commodity ceases to be the individual product of an independent craftsman who does many different things. It becomes the social product of a union of craftsmen, each of whom performs continuously only one of the partial operations that make up the whole.

Looking more closely, it is clear in the first place that a worker who spends his entire life performing one and the same simple operation turns his whole body into the automatic, specialized instrument of that operation. He therefore takes less time to perform it than a craftsman who carries out a whole series of operations in succession. The collective worker who forms the living mechanism of manufacture is composed entirely of such specialized detail workers. Compared with independent handicraft, then, more is produced in less time, which is to say that the productive power of labour rises. Furthermore, once a fragment of the work becomes the exclusive function of one person, its methods are perfected. The constant repetition of the same simple act, and the concentration of attention upon it, teach the worker through experience how to achieve the desired result with the least effort. And because several generations of workers are always alive at the same time and working together on the manufacture of a given article, the technical skill acquired in this way becomes established, accumulates, and is handed down. Manufacture develops the skill of the detail worker by taking the differentiation of trades that had grown up naturally in society at large, reproducing it inside the workshop, and systematically driving it to an extreme.

A contemporary account of India makes the point: "The muslins of Dhaka have never been surpassed in fineness, nor the calicoes and other cloths of the Coromandel Coast in the brilliance and durability of their colours. Yet they are produced without capital, machinery, division of labour, or any of the means that give such advantages to the manufacturing interests of Europe. The weaver is a solitary individual who weaves a cloth when a customer orders one, on a loom of the crudest construction, sometimes consisting of nothing more than a few branches or bars of wood roughly fastened together. There is not even a device for rolling up the warp, the lengthwise threads of the fabric. The loom must therefore be kept stretched to its full length, and it becomes so awkwardly large that it cannot fit inside the weaver's hut, so he is forced to work in the open air, where every change in the weather interrupts him. Only the special skill accumulated from generation to generation, and passed from father to son, gives the Hindu weaver, as it gives the spider, this mastery." And yet the work of such a Hindu weaver is highly complex compared with that of a worker in a manufactory.

A craftsman who performs, one after another, the various partial operations involved in producing a finished article must sometimes change his position and sometimes change his tools. Each shift from one operation to the next interrupts the flow of his labour and opens gaps, so to speak, in his working day. These gaps close as soon as he is tied to a single operation all day long; they disappear in proportion as the changes in his work diminish. The resulting gain in productive power comes either from a greater intensity of labour, since more labour power is expended in a given time, or from a reduction in the amount of labour power consumed unproductively. Every passage from rest to motion demands a certain expenditure of energy, and that expenditure ceases once the worker has maintained his normal working speed for some time. On the other hand, unbroken labour of a single uniform kind wears down the intensity and flow of a person's animal spirits, the vital energy that finds refreshment and pleasure in a simple change of activity.

The productiveness of labour depends not only on the skill of the worker but also on the perfection of his tools. Tools of the same kind, such as knives, drills, gimlets, and hammers, may be used in different processes, and a single instrument may be put to several uses within one process. But once the different operations of a labour process are separated from one another, and each partial operation takes on, in the hands of the detail worker, a form suited to it and therefore peculiar to it, the tools that formerly did several jobs must be altered. The direction of this change is set by the difficulties that arise when the tool keeps its old, unchanged form. Manufacture is distinguished by the differentiation of the instruments of labour. In Birmingham alone, five hundred varieties of hammer are produced, and each is not only adapted to one particular process; often several varieties are used exclusively for the different operations within a single process. Manufacture simplifies, improves, and multiplies the tools of labour by adapting them to the exclusive, specialized functions of each detail worker. In doing so, it creates one of the material conditions for the existence of machinery, which consists of a combination of simple instruments.

The detail worker and his tools are the simplest elements of manufacture. We turn now to manufacture as a whole.

The organization of manufacture takes two fundamentally different forms, and these play very different parts, especially in the later transformation of manufacture into modern machine industry. This double character arises from the nature of the article produced. Either the article results from the merely mechanical assembly of partial products made independently of one another, or it owes its finished shape to a series of connected processes and manipulations.

A locomotive, for example, consists of more than five thousand independent parts. It cannot, however, illustrate the first kind of true manufacture, because it is a product of modern industry. A watch can. Once the individual work of a craftsman of Nuremberg, the watch has become the social product of an immense number of detail workers: mainspring makers, dial makers, makers of the spiral hairspring, makers of jewelled holes, makers of ruby levers, hand makers, case makers, screw makers, and gilders. There are further subdivisions: wheel makers, with brass and steel wheels made separately; pin makers; movement makers; the *acheveur de pignon*, who fixes the wheels on their axles and polishes the facets; pivot makers; the *planteur de finissage*, who sets the wheels and springs into the works; the *finisseur de barillet*, who cuts the teeth in the wheels and bores the holes to the right size; escapement makers; makers of cylinders for cylinder escapements; makers of escapement wheels; makers of balance wheels; makers of the *raquette*, the device that regulates the watch's speed; and the *planteur d'échappement*, the escapement maker proper. Then come the *repasseur de barillet*, who finishes the drum that houses the mainspring; steel polishers, wheel polishers, and screw polishers; painters of numerals; dial enamellers, who melt the enamel onto the copper; the *fabricant de pendants*, who makes the ring by which the case hangs; the *finisseur de charnière*, who sets the brass hinge in the cover; the *faiseur de secret*, who fits the springs that open the case; the engraver, the chaser, the case polisher, and many more. Last of all comes the *repasseur*, who assembles the whole watch and hands it over in working order. Only a few parts of the watch pass through more than one pair of hands, and all these scattered parts come together at last in the hand that binds them into a single mechanical whole.

Because the relation between the finished product and its many different elements is purely external, it is a matter of chance, here as with all similar finished articles, whether the detail workers are brought together in one workshop or not. The detail operations may be carried on as so many independent handicrafts, as they are in the cantons of Vaud and Neuchâtel, while in Geneva there are large watch factories in which the detail workers labour together under the control of a single capitalist. Even there, the dial, the springs, and the case are seldom made in the factory itself. In the watch trade, running the business as a manufacture with workers concentrated in one place is profitable only under exceptional conditions. Competition is fiercer among workers who wish to work at home; the breaking up of the work into many dissimilar processes allows little shared use of the instruments of labour; and the capitalist, by dispersing the work, saves the expense of workshops and the like. Nevertheless, the position of this detail worker, who works at home but for a capitalist, is very different from that of the independent craftsman who works for his own customers.

The second kind of manufacture, its perfected form, produces articles that pass through connected phases of development, through a series of processes step by step. The wire in needle manufacture is an example: it passes through the hands of seventy-two, and sometimes even ninety-two, detail workers.

If we follow a particular batch of raw material, such as rags in paper manufacture or wire in needle manufacture, we see it pass in succession through a series of stages in the hands of the various detail workers until it is complete. If, on the other hand, we look at the workshop as a whole, we see the raw material present in every stage of its production at the same moment. With one set of his many hands, armed with one kind of tool, the collective worker draws the wire; with another set, armed with different tools, he simultaneously straightens it; with another he cuts it; with another he points it; and so on. The result is a greater quantity of finished commodities in a given time. Manufacture achieves this social organization of the labour process only by riveting each worker to a single fragmentary task.

Since the partial product of each detail worker is at the same time only one stage in the development of a single finished article, each worker or group of workers prepares the raw material for another worker or group. What one produces is the starting point for another. The labour time each partial process needs to reach the desired result is learned through experience, and the mechanism of manufacture as a whole rests on the assumption that a given result will be achieved in a given time. Only on this assumption can the various complementary processes run without interruption, simultaneously and side by side. This direct dependence of the operations on one another, and therefore of the workers on one another, compels each of them to spend no more than the necessary time on his work. It thereby produces a continuity, uniformity, regularity, order, and above all an intensity of labour of an entirely different kind from anything found in independent handicraft, or even in simple cooperation.

Different operations, however, take unequal lengths of time, and so in equal periods they yield unequal quantities of partial products. If the same worker is to perform the same operation day after day, there must therefore be a different number of workers for each operation, a number precisely matched to the relations among the operations. In type manufacture, for instance, there are four founders and two breakers for every rubber: the founder casts 2,000 pieces of type an hour, the breaker breaks off 4,000, and the rubber polishes 8,000.

Once experiment has established the most suitable proportion among the numbers of detail workers in the various groups for production on a given scale, that scale can be enlarged only by employing a multiple of each group. In type manufacture, it is impossible to add a single extra rubber without at the same time adding two extra breakers and four extra founders. There is a further consideration. Certain kinds of work can be done by the same individual just as well on a large scale as on a small one: the labour of supervision, for instance, or the carrying of partial products from one stage to the next. Separating such functions and assigning each to a particular worker becomes advantageous only after the number of workers has increased, and that increase must at once affect every group in proportion.

In many manufactures, however, the group itself is an organized body of labour. Take the manufacture of glass bottles. It can be broken down into three fundamentally different stages. First comes the preliminary stage: preparing the ingredients of the glass, mixing the sand, lime, and other materials, and melting them into a fluid mass. Various detail workers are employed in this first stage, as they are in the final stage of removing the bottles from the drying furnace, sorting them, packing them, and so on. Between these two stages comes the glassmaking proper, the working of the molten mass. At each mouth of the furnace works a group called "the hole," made up of one bottlemaker or finisher, one blower, one gatherer, one putter-up or whetter-off, and one taker-in. These five detail workers are so many specialized organs of a single working body that acts only as a whole, and so can function only through the direct cooperation of all five. If even one member is missing, the whole body is paralysed. But a glass furnace has several openings, four to six in England, each holding an earthenware melting pot full of molten glass, and each employing a similar five-member group. The organization within each group rests directly on the division of labour, but the link between the different groups is simple cooperation, which, by sharing one of the means of production, the furnace, uses it more economically. Such a furnace with its four to six groups makes up a glasshouse, and a glass manufactory comprises several glasshouses, together with the equipment and workers required for the preparatory and final stages.

Finally, manufacture can develop into a combination of several manufactures. The larger English glassmakers, for instance, make their own earthenware melting pots, because the success or failure of the process depends largely on their quality. In the same way, the manufacture of flint glass is combined with glass cutting and with brass founding, the latter supplying the metal mountings for various glass articles. The manufactures thus combined form more or less separate departments of a larger manufacture, yet each remains an independent process with its own division of labour. Despite the many advantages this combination offers, it never grows into a complete technical system on its own foundation. That happens only when it is transformed into an industry carried on by machinery.

Early in the manufacturing period, the principle of reducing the labour time necessary to produce commodities was recognized and stated, and here and there machines came into use, especially for certain simple preliminary processes that had to be carried out on a very large scale and with the application of great force. Thus, early in the history of paper manufacture, the tearing of rags was done in paper mills, and in metalworking the crushing of ore was done in stamping mills. The Roman Empire had handed down the elementary form of all machinery in the waterwheel. The handicraft period bequeathed to us the great inventions of the compass, gunpowder, printing with movable type, and the automatic clock. On the whole, however, machinery played a subordinate part compared with the division of labour. Its scattered use gave the great mathematicians of the age a practical foundation, and a spur, for creating the science of mechanics.

The collective worker, formed by combining a number of detail workers, is the machinery characteristic of the manufacturing period. The various operations that the producer of a commodity performs in turn make different demands on him. One requires more strength, another more skill, another more attention, and the same individual does not possess all these qualities in equal measure. Once manufacture has separated the various operations, made them independent, and isolated them, the workers are divided, classified, and grouped according to their dominant qualities. If their natural endowments are the foundation on which the division of labour is built, manufacture, once established, also develops in them new powers that are by their very nature suited only to limited and specialized functions. The collective worker now possesses, in equal excellence, every quality production requires, and he expends them in the most effective way by employing each of his organs, which consist of particular workers or groups of workers, exclusively in its own special function. The one-sidedness and the shortcomings of the detail worker become virtues once he is part of the collective worker. The habit of doing only one thing turns him into an instrument that never fails, while his connection with the whole mechanism forces him to work with the regularity of the parts of a machine.

Since the collective worker has functions both simple and complex, both high and low, his members, the individual labour powers, require different degrees of training and must therefore have different values. Manufacture thus develops a hierarchy of labour powers, matched by a scale of wages. Every process of production, however, also requires certain simple manipulations that anyone is capable of performing. These too are now cut off from their connection with the more demanding moments of the work and hardened into the exclusive functions of specially assigned workers. In every handicraft it takes over, therefore, manufacture creates a class of so-called unskilled workers, a class that handicraft industry had strictly excluded. Alongside the hierarchical gradation there appears the simple division of workers into skilled and unskilled. For the unskilled, the cost of apprenticeship disappears entirely; for the skilled, it shrinks compared with that of the old craftsmen, because their functions have been simplified. In both cases the value of labour power falls. The exception to this law occurs whenever the breaking down of the labour process gives rise to new and comprehensive functions that had either no place at all, or only a very modest one, in handicrafts.

The division of labour in manufacture that we have just described was a continuation of the division of labour that has gone on since the earliest records of history, and which, before the age of manufacture, found its fullest expression in handicraft. The new division brought about by capital clearly showed many analogies with the older one, and the two processes acted upon each other. Yet they remain essentially different. On one side stands the division of labour known for many centuries, which organized workers into the various handicrafts; on the other stands the division that capital creates within one and the same workshop. The analogy seems most undeniable where an invisible bond unites several branches of trade. The cattle-breeder produces hides, the tanner turns the hides into leather, and the shoemaker turns the leather into boots. Each of them produces only a step toward the final form, which is the product of all their labours combined. Behind them stand all the other industries that supply the cattle-breeder, the tanner, and the shoemaker with their means of production. But what binds together the independent labours of these three men? The fact that each of their products is a commodity. What, by contrast, defines the division of labour in manufacture? The fact that the detail worker produces no commodity at all. Only the common product of all the detail workers becomes a commodity.

The division of labour in society is brought about through the buying and selling of the products of different industries. The connection between the detail operations in a workshop, however, arises because several workers have sold their labour power to a single capitalist, who applies it as a combined labour power. The division of labour in the workshop presupposes that the means of production are concentrated in the hands of one capitalist; the division of labour in society presupposes that they are scattered among many independent producers of commodities. Inside the workshop, an iron law of proportion assigns a fixed number of workers to fixed functions. In the society outside it, chance and caprice decide how producers and their means of production are distributed among the various branches of industry. The division of labour within the workshop rests on the undisputed authority of the capitalist over people who are nothing more than parts of a mechanism he owns. The division of labour within society brings together independent producers of commodities who recognize no authority except competition, the pressure that their conflicting interests exert upon one another. The same bourgeois mind that praises the division of labour in the workshop, that praises the lifelong attachment of the worker to a single partial operation and his complete subjection to capital, as an organization of labour that raises its productivity, also denounces with equal vigour every conscious attempt to control and regulate production socially. Such attempts, it says, violate sacred things: the rights of property, freedom, and the unrestricted play of the individual capitalist's inclinations. It is telling that the most enthusiastic defenders of the factory system can find nothing more damning to say against a general organization of society's labour than that it would turn all of society into one immense factory.

The rules of the guilds strictly limited the number of apprentices and journeymen that a single master could employ, and in doing so they prevented him from becoming a capitalist. Nor could he employ his journeymen in any handicraft other than the one in which he himself was a master. The guilds jealously fought off every encroachment by merchants' capital, the only form of free capital they encountered. A merchant could buy any kind of commodity except labour. He was merely tolerated, as a dealer in the products of the handicrafts. When circumstances demanded a further division of labour, the existing guilds split into subdivisions or founded new guilds alongside the old ones, but they never gathered several handicrafts together in a single workshop. However much the guild system contributed to the material conditions for manufacture, by separating, isolating, and perfecting the handicrafts, it therefore ruled out the division of labour within the workshop. On the whole, the worker and his means of production stayed closely joined, like a snail and its shell. What was missing was the chief foundation of manufacture: the separation of the worker from his means of production, and the conversion of those means into capital.

The division of labour in society at large, whether or not it is brought about through the exchange of commodities, is common to the most varied economic formations of society. The division of labour within the workshop, as manufacture practises it, is a creation peculiar to the capitalist mode of production alone.

Once manufacture has been introduced, every further advance in the division of labour requires an increase in the capital held by the individual capitalist. As we have seen, the established division of labour dictates the minimum number of workers that a given capitalist must employ. Recall the example of type-founding. For every rubber, the worker who smooths the finished type, there must be two breakers, who snap off the excess metal, and four founders, who cast the type. The capitalist must employ at least these seven workers to keep his foundry running, and any expansion of his business requires at least seven more. This in turn demands a corresponding increase in tools and materials, in workshops, furnaces, and the like, and above all in raw materials, the demand for which grows faster than the number of workers. Expanding the business raises the productive power of labour, so that the same number of workers works up a larger quantity of raw material in the same span of time. That raw material must be on hand in the capitalist's possession. In the same proportion as manufacture expands, therefore, the necessities of life and the means of production that exist in society must be converted into capital in the hands of the capitalist.

The economist Heinrich Storch put it this way in his *Cours d'économie politique*: "It is not sufficient that the capital required for the subdivision of handicrafts should be in readiness in the society: it must also be accumulated in the hands of the employers in sufficiently large quantities to enable them to conduct their operations on a large scale. The more the division increases, the more does the constant employment of a given number of labourers require a greater outlay of capital in tools, raw material, and so on." Where Storch wrote "capital," he should have said the necessary means of subsistence and of production.

Manufacture, like simple cooperation, owes its existence to capital. The productive power that arises from combining labour therefore appears to be the productive power of capital. But there is an essential difference between simple cooperation and manufacture. Simple cooperation leaves the individual's way of working largely unchanged. Manufacture revolutionizes it completely and seizes labour power at its very roots. It turns the worker into a crippled monstrosity by forcing the growth of a single specialized dexterity at the expense of a whole world of productive capacities and instincts, much as the cattle-ranchers of the Río de la Plata in South America slaughter an entire animal merely for its hide or its tallow. Not only is the detail work divided among different individuals; the individual himself is made into the automatic motor of a fractional operation. The absurd fable of Menenius Agrippa, the Roman patrician who calmed the rebellious plebeians by comparing the citizens of the state to the separate limbs of a single body, becomes literal fact: a man is reduced to a mere fragment of his own body. At first the worker sells his labour power to capital because he lacks the material means of producing a commodity himself. Now his labour power will not function at all unless it has been sold to capital. It can be exercised only in the surroundings that exist in the capitalist's workshop after the sale. Unfitted by nature to make anything on his own, the manufacturing worker becomes productive only as an appendage of the capitalist's workshop. As Storch observed: "The workman who is capable of performing an entire handicraft can everywhere exercise his industry and find the means of subsistence; the other," meaning the manufacturing worker, "is but an accessory, who, separated from his comrades, is no longer either capable or independent, and is compelled to accept any law which the employer may find it convenient to impose on him."

The knowledge, judgment, and will that the independent peasant or craftsman exercises, however modestly, are now required only of the workshop as a whole. The detail workers lose the intellectual powers of production, which become concentrated in the capital that employs them. It is a consequence of the division of labour in manufacture that the individual worker is stripped of these intellectual powers, which then confront him as the property of another and as a power that rules over him. This separation begins in simple cooperation, where the capitalist represents, to each single worker, the unity and the will of the associated labour. It develops further in manufacture, which cuts the worker down into a detail worker. It is completed in modern industry, which makes science a productive force distinct from labour and presses it into the service of capital.

In manufacture, if the collective worker, and through him capital, is to grow rich in social productive power, each individual worker must be made poor in individual productive powers. "Ignorance is the mother of industry as well as of superstition," wrote J. D. Tuckett in his *History of the Past and Present State of the Labouring Population* of 1846. "Reflection and fancy are subject to err; but a habit of moving the hand or the foot is independent of either. Manufactures, accordingly, prosper most where the mind is least consulted, and where the workshop may be considered as an engine, the parts of which are men." In fact, in the middle of the eighteenth century a few manufacturers preferred to employ half-witted people for certain operations that were trade secrets.

Adam Smith, in the fifth book of *The Wealth of Nations*, published in 1776, gave a vivid description of the intellectual crippling that manufacture produces: "The understandings of the greater part of men are necessarily formed by their ordinary employments. The man whose whole life is spent in performing a few simple operations has no occasion to exert his understanding. He generally becomes as stupid and ignorant as it is possible for a human creature to become."

The body of the detail worker is crippled as well, and so manufacture is the first system to supply the material for industrial pathology and to set that science in motion. As David Urquhart wrote in his *Familiar Words* of 1855: "To subdivide a man is to execute him, if he deserves the sentence, to assassinate him if he does not. The subdivision of labour is the assassination of a people."

Cooperation based on the division of labour, which is to say manufacture, begins as a spontaneous formation. As soon as it gains some stability and reach, it becomes the recognized, methodical, and systematic form of capitalist production. History shows how the division of labour peculiar to manufacture in the strict sense first arrives at its best-adapted form through experience, behind the backs of the people involved, and then, like the guild handicrafts, strives to hold on to that form once found, sometimes succeeding for centuries. Apart from trivial matters, any change in this form results solely from a revolution in the instruments of labour. Modern manufacture in the large towns where it arises (and here I do not mean modern industry based on machinery) either finds the *disjecta membra poetae*, Horace's "scattered limbs of the poet," lying ready to hand and needing only to be gathered together, as in the manufacture of clothing, or else it can easily apply the principle of division simply by assigning each of the operations of a handicraft, such as bookbinding, exclusively to particular workers. In such cases a week's experience is enough to fix the proportion between the numbers of workers needed for the various functions.

The division of labour in manufacture thus creates a definite organization of society's labour, and in doing so develops new productive forces in society. In its specifically capitalist form, and under the given conditions it could take no other, manufacture is simply a particular method of producing relative surplus-value, of increasing the self-expansion of capital at the worker's expense. It raises the social productive power of labour for the benefit of the capitalist rather than the worker, and it does so by crippling the individual workers. It creates new conditions for the rule of capital over labour. If, on the one hand, it appears historically as progress, on the other it is a refined and civilized method of exploitation.

Political economy, which first arose as an independent science during the period of manufacture, sees in the social division of labour only a means of producing more commodities with a given quantity of labour, and therefore of cheapening commodities and accelerating the accumulation of capital. In sharp contrast to this emphasis on quantity and exchange-value stands the attitude of the writers of classical antiquity, who cared exclusively for quality and use-value. In their view, the separation of the social branches of production means that goods are better made, that the various inclinations and talents of men find a suitable field, and that without some such limitation nothing of importance can be achieved anywhere.

During the manufacturing period proper, the period in which manufacture was the dominant form of capitalist production, many obstacles stood in the way of the full development of its characteristic tendencies. Manufacture, as we have seen, does create a simple division of workers into skilled and unskilled, alongside their hierarchical arrangement into grades, yet the number of unskilled workers remained very limited. It does adapt the detail operations to the different degrees of maturity, strength, and development of its living instruments of labour, and so encourages the exploitation of women and children, yet this tendency as a whole foundered on the habits and resistance of the male workers. The splitting up of handicrafts does lower the cost of training a worker, and thereby lowers his value, yet the more difficult detail work still required a longer apprenticeship, and even where such an apprenticeship would have been unnecessary, the workers jealously insisted on it. In England, for instance, the laws of apprenticeship, with their seven years' probation, remained in full force until the end of the manufacturing period and were not cast aside until the arrival of modern industry. Because handicraft skill is the foundation of manufacture, capital is constantly forced to struggle against the insubordination of the workers. Throughout the whole manufacturing period, therefore, the complaint of the workers' lack of discipline runs like a refrain. From the sixteenth century until the epoch of modern industry, capital never managed to take command of the whole disposable working time of the manufacturing workers, and manufactures had to move from one country to another as the workers emigrated or immigrated.

At the same time, manufacture was unable either to take hold of social production in its full extent or to revolutionize it to its core. One of its most finished creations was the workshop that produced the instruments of labour themselves, above all the complicated mechanical apparatus already in use. This workshop, itself a product of the division of labour in manufacture, produced in its turn machines. With that, the fetters fell away that the dependence of work on the personal skill of the workers had still placed on the dominion of capital.

### (C) Machinery and Modern Industry

When capital employs machinery, its purpose is to cheapen commodities and, by shortening the part of the working day in which the worker works for himself, to lengthen the other part, which he gives to the capitalist without an equivalent. Machinery, in short, is a means of producing surplus-value.

In manufacture, the revolution in the mode of production begins with labour power; in modern industry it begins with the instruments of labour. Our first question, then, is how the instruments of labour are converted from tools into machines.

Mathematicians and mechanical engineers call a tool a simple machine and a machine a complex tool. They see no essential difference between the two. And it is true that every machine is a combination of simple tools, however disguised they may be. From an economic standpoint, though, this explanation is worthless. Another common explanation holds that in a tool the motive power is a human being, while in a machine it is something other than a human being: an animal, water, wind, and so on. By this reasoning a plough drawn by oxen, a device found in the most varied epochs, would be a machine, while Claussen's circular loom, which, worked by a single labourer, weaves 96,000 picks a minute, would be a mere tool. Indeed, the very same loom would be a tool when worked by hand and a machine when driven by steam. And since harnessing animal power is among humanity's earliest inventions, production by machinery would have come before production by handicraft.

All fully developed machinery consists of three essentially different parts: the motor mechanism, the transmitting mechanism, and finally the tool or working machine. The motor mechanism sets the whole in motion. Either it generates its own motive power, like the steam engine, the caloric or hot-air engine, or the electromagnetic machine, or it receives its impulse from a natural force already at hand, as the water-wheel does from a head of water or the windmill from the wind. The transmitting mechanism, made up of flywheels, shafting, toothed wheels, pulleys, straps, ropes, bands, pinions, and gearing of every kind, regulates the motion, changes its form where necessary, for instance from linear to circular, and divides and distributes it among the working machines. These first two parts exist solely to set the working machines in motion, and through that motion the working machines take hold of the material and shape it as required. The tool or working machine is the part of the machinery with which the industrial revolution of the eighteenth century began. To this day it remains the point of departure whenever a handicraft or a manufacture is turned into an industry carried on by machinery.

If we look more closely at the working machine proper, we generally find in it, though often in a much altered form, the apparatus and tools used by the craftsman or the manufacturing worker. Either the whole machine is a more or less modified mechanical version of the old handicraft tool, as with the power-loom, or the working parts mounted in the machine's frame are old acquaintances: spindles, needles, saws, and knives. The machine proper, then, is a mechanism that, once set in motion, performs with its tools the same operations that the worker formerly performed with similar tools. Whether the motive power comes from a human being or from another machine makes no difference here. From the moment the tool itself is taken out of human hands and fitted into a mechanism, a machine has replaced a mere implement.

The difference is obvious at once, even where a human being remains the prime mover. The number of implements a person can use at the same time is limited by the number of his own natural instruments of production, his bodily organs. In Germany, attempts were first made to have one spinner work two spinning wheels at once, using both hands and both feet. This proved too difficult. Later a treadle spinning wheel with two spindles was invented, but skilled spinners who could spin two threads at once were almost as rare as two-headed men. The spinning jenny, by contrast, spun with twelve to eighteen spindles from its very beginning, and the stocking-loom knits with many thousands of needles at once.

From the very outset, the number of tools a machine can bring into play simultaneously is freed from the limits that confine the tools of a craftsman.

The steam engine itself, as it was when invented during the manufacturing period at the end of the seventeenth century, and as it remained until about 1780, gave rise to no industrial revolution. On the contrary, it was the invention of machines that made a revolution in the form of the steam engine necessary.

The machine that launched the Industrial Revolution replaces the worker who handles a single tool with a mechanism that operates many similar tools at once, all driven by a single motive power, whatever form that power may take. As Charles Babbage wrote in 1832, "The union of all these simple instruments, set in motion by a single motor, constitutes a machine."

As machines grow larger and carry more working tools, they require a more massive mechanism to drive them, and that mechanism in turn needs a power greater than any human being can supply to overcome its resistance. Besides, a human being is a very imperfect instrument for producing uniform, continuous motion. Natural forces can now take his place as the source of motion, and a single prime mover can drive many machines at once.

Spinning mules and steam engines existed before there were any workers whose sole occupation was building them, just as people wore clothes before anyone made a living as a tailor. Yet the inventions of Vaucanson, Arkwright, Watt, and others could be put into practice only because these inventors found a sizable body of skilled mechanics already at hand, a workforce supplied to them by the manufacturing period. As inventions multiplied and demand for the new machines grew, machine-building split into more and more independent branches, and the division of labor within each branch grew steadily more refined. Here, in manufacture, lies the immediate technical foundation of modern industry. Manufacture produced the very machinery with which modern industry abolished both the handicraft and manufacturing systems in the fields of production it first conquered.

The factory system was therefore built, as a matter of course, on an inadequate foundation. Modern industry could not reach its full development so long as its defining instrument, the machine, owed its existence to personal strength and personal skill. It depended on the muscle, the sharp eyesight, and the manual dexterity with which the specialized workers of manufacture and the laborers of the handicrafts wielded their puny tools. Machines made this way were expensive. Beyond that, the expansion of mechanized industry, and the spread of machinery into new branches of production, depended on the growth of a class of workers whose craft was almost an art, and whose numbers could therefore increase only gradually, not by leaps and bounds. More than this, at a certain stage of its development modern industry became technologically incompatible with the foundation that handicraft and manufacture had laid for it. Machine-building confronted tasks that manufacture could not perform. Machines such as the modern hydraulic press, the modern power loom, and the modern carding engine could never have been produced by manufacturing methods.

A radical change in the mode of production in one branch of industry forces similar changes in others. Mechanized spinning made mechanized weaving a necessity, and together they made a mechanical and chemical revolution in bleaching, printing, and dyeing unavoidable. In the same way, the revolution in cotton spinning called forth the cotton gin, which separates the seeds from the fiber; only with this invention could cotton be grown on the enormous scale now required. Above all, the revolution in the methods of production in industry and agriculture demanded a revolution in communication and transport. The means of communication and transport inherited from the manufacturing period soon became intolerable shackles on modern industry, with its feverish pace of production, its vast scale, its constant hurling of capital and labor from one branch of production into another, and its newly forged connections to markets across the entire world. So, apart from radical changes in the construction of sailing ships, communication and transport were gradually adapted to the methods of mechanized industry through a system of river steamers, railways, ocean steamships, and telegraphs. But the enormous masses of iron that now had to be forged, welded, cut, bored, and shaped required machines of colossal size, and the methods of the manufacturing period were utterly inadequate for building them.
