Modern industry therefore had to take charge of producing its own characteristic instrument, the machine, and to build machines by means of machines.

If we now turn our attention to the part of machine-building machinery that does the actual work, the operating tool, we find the old hand implements reappearing, but on a gigantic scale. The boring machine works with an immense drill driven by a steam engine, and without it the cylinders of large steam engines and hydraulic presses could not be made at all. The mechanical lathe is simply a giant version of the ordinary foot-powered lathe. The planing machine is an iron carpenter, working on iron with the same tools a human carpenter uses on wood. The instrument that cuts veneers on the London wharves is a gigantic razor. The blade of the shearing machine, which slices iron as easily as a tailor's scissors cut cloth, is a monstrous pair of shears. And the steam hammer works with an ordinary hammerhead, but one so heavy that not even Thor could swing it. One such hammer weighs more than six tons and falls vertically seven feet onto an anvil weighing thirty-six tons. Pulverizing a block of granite is child's play for it, yet it can just as easily drive a nail into a piece of soft wood with a series of light taps.

In simple cooperation, and even in cooperation based on the division of labor, the replacement of the isolated worker by the collective worker still appears more or less accidental. Machinery, with a few exceptions to be discussed later, operates only through associated labor, labor performed in common. With machinery, the cooperative character of labor becomes a technical necessity dictated by the instrument of labor itself.

We saw earlier that the productive forces arising from cooperation and the division of labor cost capital nothing. They are the natural forces of social labor. Physical forces such as steam and water likewise cost nothing when they are put to work in production. But just as a person needs lungs in order to breathe, he needs some product of human hands in order to make productive use of physical forces. A waterwheel is needed to exploit the force of falling water, and a steam engine to exploit the expansive power of steam. Science stands in the same position as the forces of nature. Once discovered, the law by which a magnetic needle is deflected in the field of an electric current, or the law by which iron becomes magnetized when a current circulates around it, costs not a penny. But exploiting these laws for telegraphy and similar purposes requires costly and elaborate apparatus. It is obvious at first glance that modern industry, by incorporating both tremendous physical forces and the natural sciences into the production process, raises the productivity of labor to an extraordinary degree. It is by no means equally obvious that this increased productive power is not purchased, on the other side of the ledger, by a greater expenditure of labor. Machinery, like every other component of constant capital, creates no new value; it only passes its own value on to the product it helps to make. And it is as clear as day that machines and systems of machinery, the characteristic instruments of modern industry, carry incomparably more value than the tools of handicraft and manufacture. Far from cheapening the product, they make it more expensive in proportion to their own value.

It should be noted, however, that a machine never adds more value to any individual product than it loses, on average, through wear and tear. There is thus a large gap between the total value of a machine and the fraction of that value it transfers to the product at any one time. The longer the machine lasts, the smaller that fraction becomes. This holds true for every implement of labor and every instrument of production. But the difference between a machine's use and its wear and tear is greater than the corresponding difference for a hand tool. A machine is built of more durable material and therefore lasts longer; its operation, governed by strict scientific laws, allows for greater economy; and, finally, its range of production is far larger than that of a tool.

In a lecture published in 1858, Mr. Baynes of Blackburn estimated that "each real mechanical horse-power will drive 450 self-acting mule spindles, with preparation, or 200 throstle spindles, or 15 looms for 40 inch cloth with the appliances for warping, sizing, &c." (The mule and the throstle were two types of spinning machine.) In the first case, the daily cost of one horsepower, together with the wear and tear on the machinery it drives, is spread over the day's output of 450 mule spindles; in the second, over the output of 200 throstle spindles; in the third, over the output of 15 power looms. As a result, only a minute amount of value from wear and tear is transferred to any single pound of yarn or yard of cloth. The same is true of the steam hammer described above. Because its daily wear and tear, its coal consumption, and its other costs are spread over the enormous quantities of iron it hammers in a day, it adds only a small amount of value to each hundredweight of iron. That value would be very great, however, if this giant instrument were used to drive in nails.

When we examined cooperation and manufacture, we saw that certain conditions of production, such as buildings, suffer less wear and tear because they are used in common, and so add only slightly to the cost of the product. With machinery this saving grows still larger. Not only is a single working machine consumed in common by its many operating tools, but a single motor, along with part of the transmission apparatus, is consumed in common by many working machines.

Given the rate at which machinery transfers its value to the product, the amount of value transferred depends on the machinery's total value. The less labor a machine contains, the less value it passes on to the product. And the less value it gives up, the more productive it is, and the more closely its services approach those of the forces of nature.

Clearly, if it costs as much labor to produce a machine as the machine saves in use, then labor has merely been shifted from one place to another. The total labor required to produce a commodity has not been reduced, and the productivity of labor has not increased. Yet the difference between the labor a machine costs and the labor it saves does not depend on the difference between the machine's value and the value of the tool it replaces. That difference persists as long as the labor spent on the machine, and therefore the portion of its value that it adds to the product, remains smaller than the value the worker added to the product with his tool. The productivity of a machine is therefore measured by the human labor power it replaces.

The labor a machine saves, however, must not be confused with wages. Suppose a machine costs as much as a year's wages for the 150 workers it displaces, say £3,000. That £3,000 is by no means the monetary expression of all the labor those 150 workers added to their product before the machine arrived. It expresses only the portion of their year's labor that they performed for themselves, the portion represented by their wages. They received £3,000 in wages for the year, but in return they supplied a greater value. Now, if the machine costs £3,000, and that sum includes all the labor that went into making it, however that labor was divided between wages for the workers and surplus value for the capitalist, then the value of the machine is less than the value the 150 workers formerly produced. So even when a machine costs as much as the labor power it displaces, the labor embodied in it is still far less than the living labor it replaces.

If the only aim were to cheapen products, using a machine would pay as long as producing it took less labor than its use made unnecessary. Some figures will illustrate the point. In the case above, 150 workers received £3,000 a year in wages and supplied in return, let us say, £6,000 worth of labor, so that surplus value amounted to 100 percent of their wages. As long as building the machine that does the work of these 150 men costs less than £6,000, using it would benefit society, since it saves labor. But the capitalist cannot calculate this way. He pays only £3,000 for the labor of the 150 men, so he cannot profitably use the machine once it costs more than £3,000. A communist society would therefore employ machinery on an entirely different scale from a bourgeois one. For the capitalist, only the wages he actually pays enter into his costs of production. Those wages vary from country to country for the same amount of labor, and they also vary by falling below or rising above the value of labor power.

This explains why machines are now invented in England and used only in North America, just as in the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries machines were invented in Germany to be used only in Holland, and just as many French inventions of the eighteenth century were exploited in England alone. In the older countries, machinery introduced into some branches of industry creates such a surplus of labor in other branches that wages there fall below the value of labor power, and this in turn obstructs the use of machinery in those branches. In certain branches of the English woolen industry, the employment of children has fallen considerably in recent years and in some cases disappeared entirely. The reason is that the Factory Acts required two shifts of children, one working six hours and the other four, or each working five. But the parents refused to sell the labor of these "half-timers" for less than that of the "full-timers," and so machinery was brought in to replace the half-timers. Before the law forbade the employment of women and of children under ten in the mines, capitalists considered the use of naked women and girls, often working alongside men, so thoroughly sanctioned by their moral code, and above all by their account books, that they turned to machinery only after the Act was passed. The Americans have invented a stone-breaking machine. The English do not use it, because the "wretch" who does this work is paid for so small a portion of his labor that machinery would raise the capitalist's cost of production. "Wretch," it should be said, is the accepted term in English political economy for the agricultural laborer. In England, as late as 1860, women were still sometimes used in place of horses to haul canal boats, because the labor needed to produce horses and machines is a precisely known quantity, while the labor needed to maintain the women of the surplus population falls below all calculation. Nowhere, therefore, do we find a more shameful squandering of human labor power for the most contemptible purposes than in England, the land of machinery.

## Chapter X: The Influence of Industrial Progress on the Working Classes

### (A) Labour of Women and Children

Because machinery makes muscular strength unnecessary, it becomes a means of employing workers who have little physical strength, or whose bodies are not yet fully developed but whose limbs are all the more supple. The labour of women and children was therefore the first thing that capitalists who used machinery went looking for. That powerful substitute for labour and labourers was immediately turned into a means of increasing the number of wage-workers, by bringing every member of the worker's family, regardless of age or sex, under the direct command of capital. Compulsory work for the capitalist took the place not only of children's play but also of the free, moderate labour that the family had once done at home for its own support.

The value of labour power had been determined not only by the labour time needed to maintain the individual adult worker but also by the labour time needed to maintain his family. By throwing every member of that family onto the labour market, machinery lowers the value of the man's labour power. Buying the labour power of a family of four may perhaps cost more than buying the labour power of the head of the household once did, but in return the capitalist gets four days' labour in place of one. For the family to live, four people must now not only work but also perform surplus labour for the capitalist.

As Thomas de Quincey observed in *The Logic of Political Economy*, published in London in 1845: "The numerical increase of labourers has been great, through the growing substitution of female for male, and above all, of child for adult labour. Three girls of 13, at wages of from 6 shillings to 8 shillings a week, have replaced the one man of mature age, of wages varying from 18 shillings to 45 shillings." Since certain family functions, such as nursing and breastfeeding infants, cannot be done away with entirely, mothers who have been commandeered by capital must hire substitutes of some kind. Household work such as sewing and mending must be replaced by buying ready-made goods. Less labour spent in the home therefore means more money spent. The cost of keeping the family rises and cancels out the larger income. On top of this, thrift and good judgment in buying and preparing food and other necessities become impossible.

Machinery also transforms the contract between worker and capitalist, because capital now buys children and young people who are under age. Formerly, the worker sold his own labour power, which he disposed of, at least in name, as a free agent. Now he sells his wife and child. He has become a slave dealer. Notices seeking child labour often read, in their very form, like the inquiries for Black slaves that once appeared among the advertisements in American newspapers. The reports of the Children's Employment Commission of 1864 to 1866 contain truly revolting details about how working parents conducted themselves in this traffic in children, conduct that was in every respect a form of slave-dealing.

One consequence of the breakdown of family life that follows is the enormous death rate among the children of factory workers during their first few years. In sixteen of the registration districts into which England is divided, there are on average only 9,000 deaths a year for every 100,000 living children under the age of one; in one district, only 7,000. In 24 districts the deaths number at least 10,000 but fewer than 11,000; in 30 districts, more than 11,000 but fewer than 12,000; in 48 districts, more than 12,000 but fewer than 13,000; in 22 districts, more than 20,000; in 25 districts, more than 21,000; in 17, more than 22,000; in 11, more than 23,000; in Leek, Wolverhampton, Ashton-under-Lyne, and Preston, more than 24,000; in Nottingham, Stockport, and Bradford, more than 25,000; in Wisbech, 26,000; and in Manchester, 26,125. An official medical inquiry in 1861 showed that, local causes aside, these high death rates were chiefly due to mothers being employed away from home, and to the neglect and mistreatment that followed from their absence: among other things, insufficient nourishment, unsuitable food, and dosing with opiates. Beyond this, an unnatural estrangement grows up between mother and child, leading to the deliberate starving and poisoning of children. In those agricultural districts where the employment of women is at a minimum, by contrast, the death rate is very low.

The moral degradation caused by the capitalist exploitation of women and children has been so thoroughly described by Friedrich Engels in *The Condition of the Working Class in England* and by other writers that I need only mention it here. But there is also the intellectual desolation artificially produced by turning immature human beings into mere machines for manufacturing surplus value. This condition is quite distinct from the natural ignorance that leaves the mind fallow without destroying its capacity for growth, its natural fertility. That desolation eventually forced even the English Parliament to make elementary education a compulsory condition for the "productive" employment of children under fourteen in every industry covered by the Factory Acts. The spirit of capitalist production shows plainly in the absurd wording of the so-called education clauses of the Factory Acts, in the absence of any administrative machinery to enforce them, in the manufacturers' opposition even to these clauses, and in the tricks they used to evade them. The law requires nothing more than that on certain days of the week, and for a certain number of hours (three) on each of those days, the children be shut up within the four walls of a place called a school, and that the child's employer receive each week a certificate to that effect, signed by a person whom the signer designates as a schoolmaster or schoolmistress. Before the amended Factory Act of 1844 was passed, it happened fairly often that these certificates of school attendance were signed by the schoolmaster or schoolmistress with a cross, since they themselves could not write.

As one factory inspector reported: "But it is not only in the miserable places above referred to that the children obtain certificates of school attendance without having received instruction of any value, for in many schools where there is a competent teacher, his efforts are of little avail from the distracting crowd of children of all ages, from infants of three years old and upwards; his livelihood, miserable at the best, depending on the pence received from the greatest number of children whom it is possible to cram into the space. To this is to be added scanty school furniture, deficiency of books and other materials for teaching, and the depressing effect upon the poor children themselves of a close, noisome atmosphere. I have been in many such schools, where I have seen rows of children doing absolutely nothing; and this is certified as school attendance, and, in statistical returns, such children are set down as being educated."

The treacherous way in which capitalists try to defeat the law can be seen in the following example. Under the Act covering print works and similar industries, "every child, before being employed in a print work, must have attended school for at least 30 days, and not less than 150 hours, during the six months immediately preceding such first day of employment, and during the continuance of its employment in the print works, it must attend for a like period of 30 days, and 150 hours, during every successive period of six months.... The attendance at school must be between 8 a.m. and 6 p.m. No attendance of less than two and a half hours, nor more than five hours on any one day, shall be reckoned as part of the 150 hours."

How did capital carry out these legal obligations? "Under ordinary circumstances the children attend school morning and afternoon for 30 days, for at least five hours each day, and upon the expiration of the 30 days, the statutory total of 150 hours having been attained, having, in their language, made up their book, they return to the print work, where they continue until the six months have expired, when another instalment of school attendance becomes due, and they again seek the school until the book is again made up.... Many boys having attended school for the required number of hours, when they return to school after the expiration of their six months' work in the print work, are in the same condition as when they first attended school as print-work boys. They have lost all they gained by their previous school attendance.... In other print works the children's attendance at school is made to depend altogether upon the exigencies of the work in the establishment. The requisite number of hours is made up each six months, by instalments consisting of from three to five hours at a time, spreading over, perhaps, the whole six months.... For instance, the attendance on one day might be from 8 to 11 a.m., on another day from 1 p.m. to 6 p.m., and the child might not appear at school again for several days, when it would attend from 3 p.m. to 6 p.m.; then it might attend for three or four days consecutively, or for a week, then it would not appear in school for three weeks or a month, after that upon some odd days at some odd hours when the operative who employed it chose to spare it; and thus the child was, as it were, buffeted from school to work, from work to school, until the tale of 150 hours was told."

By flooding the ranks of the workers with women and children, machinery at last breaks down the resistance that male workers had continued to offer to the despotism of capital throughout the period of manufacture.

### (B) Prolongation of the Working-Day

Machinery is the most powerful means of increasing the productivity of labour, that is, of shortening the working time needed to produce a commodity. Yet in the hands of capital it becomes the most powerful means of lengthening the working day beyond every limit set by human nature. On the one hand, it creates new conditions that allow capital to give full rein to this constant tendency of its own; on the other, it supplies new motives that sharpen capital's appetite for other people's labour.

In the form of machinery, the instruments of labour become automatic, things that move and work independently of the worker. From then on they are an industrial perpetual-motion machine that would go on producing forever if it did not run up against certain natural obstacles: the weak bodies and the strong wills of the human beings who tend it. Capital is therefore driven by a longing to reduce that resistance to a minimum. The resistance is lessened, moreover, by the apparent ease of machine work and by the more pliant and docile character of the women and children employed at it.

The longer a machine runs, the greater the mass of products over which the value it transfers is spread, and the smaller the share of that value added to each individual commodity. That alone is reason enough for the capitalist to extend the machine's daily operation as far as he can.

A machine's wear and tear is not exactly proportional to its working time. But even if it were, a machine working sixteen hours a day for seven and a half years would cover the same total working period, and transfer no more value to the total product, than the same machine working eight hours a day for fifteen years. In the first case, however, the machine's value would be reproduced twice as fast as in the second, and the capitalist using it would absorb as much surplus value in seven and a half years as he would otherwise absorb in fifteen.

A machine's physical wear and tear is of two kinds. One comes from use; the other from disuse, as a sword rusts when left in its scabbard. This second kind is caused by the elements, and to a certain degree it is inversely proportional to the machine's use: the longer the machine stands idle, the more the elements wear it down.

Beyond its physical wear and tear, a machine also undergoes what may be called a moral depreciation, a loss of value that has nothing to do with its physical condition. It loses exchange value either when machines of the same kind come to be produced more cheaply, or when better machines enter into competition with it. In both cases, however new and serviceable the machine may be, its value is no longer determined by the labour actually embodied in it, but by the labour time needed to reproduce either the machine itself or the better one. It has therefore lost value to a greater or lesser extent. The shorter the period in which its total value is reproduced, the smaller the danger of this moral depreciation; and the longer the working day, the shorter that period becomes. When machinery is first introduced into an industry, new methods of producing it more cheaply follow one after another in quick succession, and so do improvements, affecting not only individual parts and details of the machine but its entire design. This particular incentive to lengthen the working day is therefore felt most sharply in the early life of machinery.

If the length of the working day is fixed and all other circumstances remain the same, exploiting twice as many workers requires doubling not only the portion of constant capital invested in machinery and buildings, but also the portion spent on raw materials and auxiliary materials. Lengthening the working day, by contrast, allows production to expand without any change in the capital invested in machinery and buildings. This holds true, to some degree, for every extension of the working day, but in the case of machinery the effect is more pronounced, because the capital converted into instruments of labour makes up a far larger share of the whole. In 1837, Mr. Ashworth, a cotton magnate, said to Professor Nassau W. Senior: "When a labourer lays down his spade, he renders useless, for that period, a capital worth eighteenpence. When one of our people," meaning a factory worker, "leaves the mill, he renders useless a capital that has cost £100,000." Imagine it! Making a capital that cost £100,000 "useless" for even a single moment! It is truly monstrous that any one of "our people" should ever leave the factory at all! As Senior, once instructed by Ashworth, clearly understood, the growing use of machinery makes a constant lengthening of the working day "desirable."

When machines are first introduced in isolated cases into a given branch of industry, the social value of the machine-made product is higher than its individual value; that is, the machine-made product requires less labour than the product of competitors working without machines. Its value, however, is determined by the "socially necessary" labour, which in this case is the larger quantity of labour required where no machine is available. The machine-made product can therefore be sold at a price well above the value it actually embodies. During this transitional period, when the use of machinery amounts to a kind of monopoly, profits are exceptional, and the capitalist tries to make the most of "the sunny time of this his first love" by lengthening the working day as far as possible. The size of the profit whets his appetite for still more.

As machinery comes into general use in a particular industry, the social value of the product sinks to its individual value, and a law asserts itself: surplus value arises not from the labour power that machinery has replaced, but from the labour power actually employed in working the machinery. Surplus value arises from variable capital alone, that is, from living labour. It must therefore grow in proportion as capital employs living labour, and shrink in proportion as the quantity of such labour is reduced. Yet the purpose of the machine is precisely to eliminate living labour and take its place. Machinery increases productive power and cheapens the product, since it makes the product with less expenditure of labour. It thereby lowers the cost of living, and with it the value of labour power. But it achieves all these results only by reducing the number of workers a given capitalist employs; that is, by shifting part of what was formerly variable capital, spent on wages, to the purchase and upkeep of machines. It transforms variable capital into constant capital, which produces no surplus value.

A concrete example makes this clear. Before machinery was introduced, a capital of £5,000 had to be spent 40 percent on implements of labour and raw materials, with the remaining 60 percent going to pay for human labour. Once machinery is introduced, the productivity of the enterprise triples. From then on only 20 percent of the capital goes to pay for labour, and two-thirds of the workers previously employed are dismissed. The share of capital that used to pay them now goes to buy machines and the raw materials those machines will work up.

It is impossible, however, to squeeze as much surplus value out of two workers as out of twenty-four. If each of these twenty-four men gives only one hour of surplus labour out of every twelve, together they give twenty-four hours of surplus labour, while twenty-four hours is the entire labour of the two men. The application of machinery to the production of surplus value therefore contains an inherent contradiction, since it can raise the rate of surplus value only by reducing the number of workers. It is this contradiction that, in turn, drives the capitalist, without his being aware of it, to lengthen the working day excessively, so that he may make up for the smaller number of workers he exploits by extracting more absolute surplus value from each individual worker.

On the one hand, then, the capitalist use of machinery supplies new and powerful motives for lengthening the working day, and transforms methods of labour so radically as to break down all opposition to this tendency. On the other hand, partly by employing women and children and partly by setting free the workers it displaces, it produces a surplus working population that is forced to submit to the dictates of capital. Hence that remarkable phenomenon in the history of modern industry: machinery sweeps away every moral and natural limit on the length of the working day. Hence, too, the economic paradox that the most powerful instrument for shortening labour time becomes the most reliable means of placing every moment of the worker's time, and his family's, at the capitalist's disposal for the purpose of expanding the value of his capital.

The birth of machine production and modern industry in the last third of the eighteenth century was followed by an immense lengthening of the working day, as violent and sweeping as an avalanche. Every boundary of morality and nature, of age and sex, of day and night, became so confused that as late as 1860 an English judge needed the hair-splitting subtlety of a Talmudic scholar to explain "judicially" what was day and what was night. Capital celebrated its orgies. As one report put it: "The fact is, that prior to the Act of 1833, young persons and children were worked all night, all day, or both ad libitum."

Let us now look at certain branches of production in which the exploitation of labour is still free from restraint today (1863 to 1865), or was until very recently.

On 14 January 1860, Mr. Broughton Charlton, a county magistrate, chairing a meeting at the Assembly Rooms in Nottingham, declared "that there was an amount of privation and suffering among that portion of the population connected with the lace trade, unknown in other parts of the civilised world.... Children of nine or ten years are dragged from their squalid beds at two, three, or four o'clock in the morning and compelled to work for a bare subsistence until ten, eleven, or twelve at night, their limbs wearing away, their frames dwindling, their faces whitening, and their humanity absolutely sinking into a stonelike torpor, utterly horrible to contemplate.... What can be thought of a town which holds a public meeting to petition that the period of labour for men shall be diminished to eighteen hours a day?"

In the twenty-two years before 1860, the potteries of Staffordshire were the subject of three parliamentary inquiries. For my purposes it is enough to take, from the reports of 1860 and 1863, some testimony given by the exploited children themselves. From the children we can form an idea of the adults, especially the girls and women, in a branch of industry beside which cotton-spinning looks like a pleasant and healthy occupation.

William Wood, nine years old, was seven years and ten months old when he started work. He "ran moulds," carrying freshly moulded articles into the drying room and then bringing back the empty moulds. He came to work every day of the week at 6 a.m. and stopped at about 9 p.m. "I work till 9 o'clock at night six days in the week. I have done so seven or eight weeks." Fifteen hours of labour for a nine-year-old child! J. Murray, twelve years old, says: "I turn jigger," meaning he turned the wheel that drives the potter's shaping machine, "and run moulds. I come at 6. Sometimes I come at 4. I worked all night last night, till 6 o'clock this morning. I have not been in bed since the night before last. There were eight or nine other boys working last night. All but one have come this morning. I get 3 shillings and sixpence. I do not get any more for working at night. I worked two nights last week."

Dr. Greenhow reports that the average lifespan in the pottery districts of Stoke-on-Trent and Wolstanton is extraordinarily short. Although only 30.0 percent of the adult male population over twenty in Stoke, and only 30.4 percent in Wolstanton, work in the potteries, more than half of all deaths among men of that age in the first district, and nearly two-fifths in the second, result from lung diseases among the potters. Dr. Boothroyd, a medical practitioner at Hanley, says: "Each successive generation of potters is more dwarfed and less robust than the preceding one." Another doctor, Mr. M'Bean, said likewise that "since he began to practise among the potters 25 years ago, he had observed a marked degeneration, especially shown in diminution of stature and breadth." These statements come from Dr. Greenhow's report of 1860.

From the Commissioners' report of 1863 comes the following. Dr. J. T. Arledge, senior physician of the North Staffordshire Infirmary, says: "The potters as a class, both men and women, represent a degenerated population, both physically and morally. They are, as a rule, stunted in growth, ill-shaped, and frequently ill-formed in the chest; they become prematurely old, and are certainly short-lived; they are phlegmatic and bloodless, and exhibit their debility of constitution by obstinate attacks of dyspepsia, and disorders of the liver and kidneys, and by chest disease, pneumonia, phthisis," that is, tuberculosis of the lungs, "bronchitis, and asthma. One form would appear peculiar to them and is known as potter's asthma, or potter's consumption. Scrofula," a tubercular disease, "attacking the glands, or bones, or other parts of the body, is a disease of two-thirds or more of the potters.... That the 'degenerescence' of the population of this district is not even greater than it is, is due to the constant recruiting from the adjacent country, and intermarriages with more healthy races."

Mr. Charles Pearson, formerly house surgeon at the same institution, wrote to Commissioner Longe: "I can only speak from personal observation and not from statistical data, but I do not hesitate to assert that my indignation has been aroused again and again at the sight of poor children whose health has been sacrificed to gratify the avarice of either parents or employers." He lists the causes of the potters' diseases and sums them up in two words: "long hours." Everything that holds for the potteries of England holds equally for those of Scotland.

The manufacture of lucifer matches, the friction matches tipped with phosphorus, dates from 1833, when a method was discovered for applying phosphorus to the match itself. After 1845 the trade grew rapidly in England, spreading above all through the densely populated parts of London, and also through Manchester, Birmingham, Liverpool, Bristol, Norwich, Newcastle, and Glasgow. Along with it spread a form of lockjaw that a Viennese physician had identified in 1845 as a disease peculiar to match-makers. Half the workers are children under thirteen and young people under eighteen. The trade is so notorious for its unhealthiness and its sheer unpleasantness that only the most wretched part of the working class, half-starved widows and the like, will surrender their children to it: "the ragged, half-starved, untaught children." Of the witnesses Commissioner White examined in 1863, 270 were under eighteen, 50 under ten, 10 were only eight years old, and 5 only six. Their working day ran from 12 to 14 or 15 hours. There was night work, there were irregular mealtimes, and most meals were eaten in the very workrooms that reeked of phosphorus. Dante would have found the worst torments of his *Inferno* surpassed in this trade.

In the manufacture of paper-hangings, or wallpaper, the coarser kinds are printed by machine and the finer ones by hand, using carved wooden blocks. The busiest months run from the beginning of October to the end of April. Throughout that season the work goes on at a furious pace without a pause, from six in the morning to ten at night or later.

G. Apsden testified in 1862: "That boy of mine... when he was 7 years old I used to carry him on my back to and fro through the snow, and he used to have 16 hours a day.... I have often knelt down to feed him as he stood by the machine, for he could not leave it or stop." Smith, the managing partner of a Manchester factory, told the commissioners: "We" (by which he means the "hands" who work for "us") "work on, with no stoppage for meals, so that the day's work of 10½ hours is finished by 4.30 p.m., and all after that is overtime." Does this Mr. Smith himself go without a meal for 10½ hours? "We" (the same Smith) "seldom leave off working before 6 p.m.... For all these, children and adults alike" (152 children and young persons and 140 adults), "the average work for the last 18 months has been at the very least 7 days, 5 hours, or 78½ hours a week. For the six weeks ending May 2nd this year" (1863) "the average was higher — 8 days or 84 hours a week." And yet this same Mr. Smith adds with a smile, "Machine-work is not great." The employers who use hand block-printing say much the same: "Hand labour is more healthy than machine-work." On the whole, the manufacturers indignantly reject the proposal "to stop the machines at least during meal times."

In January 1866, three railwaymen stood before a London coroner's jury: a guard, an engine driver, and a signalman. A terrible railway accident had hurried hundreds of passengers into the next world, and the negligence of these employees was blamed for it. With one voice they told the jury that ten or twelve years earlier their working day had lasted only eight hours. Over the previous five or six years it had been screwed up to 14, 18, and 20 hours, and under the special pressure of holiday crowds, when excursion trains were running, it often stretched to 40 or 50 hours without a break. They were ordinary men, not Cyclopes, the tireless giants of myth who labored at the forge. At a certain point their capacity to work simply gave out. A stupor came over them. Their brains stopped thinking and their eyes stopped seeing. The thoroughly "respectable" British jurymen responded with a verdict that sent them for trial on a charge of manslaughter at the next assizes, the periodic criminal courts of the county. To this verdict they attached a gentle "rider," a note of recommendation, expressing the pious hope that the capitalist magnates of the railways would in future be more extravagant in buying a sufficient quantity of labor power, and more "abstemious," more "self-denying," more "thrifty" in draining the labor power they had paid for.

The railwaymen's own account gives an example of a very common case: "One fireman commenced work on the Monday morning at a very early hour. When he had finished what is called a day's work, he had been on duty 14 hours 50 minutes. Before he had time to get his tea, he was again called on for duty.... He worked a total of 29 hours 15 minutes without intermission. The rest of the week's work was made up as follows: Wednesday, 15 hours; Thursday, 15 hours 35 minutes; Friday, 14½ hours; Saturday, 14 hours 10 minutes, making a total for the week of 88 hours 40 minutes. Now, sir, fancy his astonishment on being paid 6 days for the whole. Thinking it was a mistake, he inquired what they considered a day's work, and was told 13 hours" (that is, 78 hours a week). "But what about the payment for the extra 10 hours and 40 minutes? After long bargaining he received tenpence."

From the motley crowd of workers in every trade, let us take two more figures whose sharp contrast proves that before capital all people are alike: a milliner and a blacksmith.

In the last week of June 1863, every daily paper in London ran a paragraph under the "sensational" heading "Death from Simple Overwork." It concerned the death of a milliner, Mary Anne Walkley, twenty years old, employed at a highly respected dressmaking establishment run by a lady with the agreeable name of Elise. The old, often-told story was told once more. This young woman had worked an average of 16½ hours a day, and during the season often 30 hours without a break, her failing strength revived now and then with doses of sherry, port, or coffee. The season was at its height. Gorgeous gowns had to be conjured up in the twinkling of an eye for the noble ladies invited to the ball in honor of the newly imported Princess of Wales. Mary Anne Walkley had worked without stopping for 26½ hours, alongside sixty other young women, thirty to a room that held only a third of the cubic feet of air they needed. At night they slept two to a bed in the stifling cubicles into which the bedroom had been divided by board partitions. And this was one of the best millinery houses in London. Mary Anne Walkley fell ill on Friday and died on Sunday, without, to Madame Elise's astonishment, having first finished the work in hand. The doctor, Mr. Keys, who was called to her deathbed too late, testified plainly to the coroner's jury that "Mary Anne Walkley had died from long hours of work in an overcrowded workroom, and a too small and badly ventilated bedroom." To teach the doctor some manners, the jury then returned a verdict that "the deceased had died of apoplexy, but there was reason to fear that her death had been accelerated by over-work in an over-crowded workroom, &c."

Dr. Richardson, senior physician at one of the London hospitals, writing in the *Social Science Review* of 18 July 1863 under the title "Work and Overwork," observed: "With needlewomen of all kinds, including milliners, dressmakers, and ordinary sempstresses, there are three miseries — over-work, deficient air, and either deficient food or deficient digestion.... But the mischiefs of the trade, in the metropolis especially, are that it is monopolised by some twenty-six capitalists.... This power tells throughout the whole class of female workers. If a dress-maker can get a little circle of customers, such is the competition that, in her home, she must work to the death to hold together, and this same over-work she must of necessity inflict on any who may assist her. If she fail, or do not try independently, she must join an establishment, where her labour is not less, but where her money is safe. Placed thus, she becomes a mere slave, tossed about with the variations of society. Now at home, in one room, starving, or near to it, then engaged 15, 16, aye, even 18 hours out of the 24, in an air that is scarcely tolerable, and on food which, even if it be good, cannot be digested in the absence of pure air. On these victims, consumption, which is purely a disease of bad air, feeds."

Dr. Richardson goes on: "It is not only in dressmakers' rooms that working to death is the order of the day, but in a thousand other places; in every place I had almost said, where 'a thriving business' has to be done.... We will take the blacksmith as a type. If the poets were true, there is no man so hearty, so merry, as the blacksmith; he rises early and strikes his sparks before the sun; he eats and drinks and sleeps as no other man. Working in moderation, he is, in fact, in one of the best of human positions, physically speaking. But we follow him into the city or town, and we see the stress of work on that strong man, and what then is his position in the death-rate of his country? In Marylebone, blacksmiths die at the rate of 31 per thousand per annum, or 11 above the mean of the male adults of the country in its entirety. The occupation, instinctive almost as a portion of human art, unobjectionable as a branch of human industry, is made by mere excess of work the destroyer of the man. He can strike so many blows per day, walk so many steps, breathe so many breaths, produce so much work, and live an average, say of fifty years; he is made to strike so many more blows, to walk so many more steps, to breathe so many more breaths per day, and to increase altogether by a fourth the work of his life. He meets the effort; the result is, that producing for a limited time a fourth more work, he dies at 37 for 50."

### (C) Intensification of Labour

The boundless lengthening of the working day, which machinery brought about in the hands of capital, eventually provoked a reaction from society, whose very sources of life were under threat. The result was a normal working day of fixed length, set by law. On the basis of that normal day, the intensity of labor rose enormously.

It is obvious that as machinery spreads, and as a special class of workers accustomed to machines accumulates experience, the speed and intensity of labor will naturally increase. In England, for half a century, the lengthening of the working day went hand in hand with the growing intensity of factory work. Yet the reader will readily see that where labor is not performed in occasional bursts but repeated day after day with unvarying regularity, a point must inevitably come at which the length of the working day and the intensity of labor exclude each other. Beyond that point, a longer day is possible only with a lower intensity, and a higher intensity only with a shorter day. Once the steadily mounting revolt of the working class forced Parliament to shorten working hours by law, and to begin by imposing a normal working day on the factories proper, any further increase in surplus value through lengthening the day was ruled out for good. From that moment, capital threw all its weight into producing surplus value by other means, chiefly by pressing ahead with the improvement of machinery.

From then on, surplus value grew not only by cheapening the product, and so lowering the value of labor power, but also by intensifying labor itself, that is, by raising the tension at which labor power was worked, so that in a shorter time it had to perform as much labor as it had previously performed in a longer one, or even more. The more intensive ten-hour day now contained as much labor, as much expended labor power, as the old "porous" twelve-hour day, a day riddled with gaps and slack. The product of one of the new hours therefore had as much value as the product of 1⅕ of the old hours, or more. Leaving aside the additional relative surplus value that comes from the greater productivity of labor, the capitalist now obtained the same mass of value from, say, 3⅓ hours of surplus labor and 6⅔ hours of necessary labor as he had previously obtained from four hours of surplus labor and eight hours of necessary labor.

This brings us to the question of how labor is intensified.

The first effect of shortening the working day follows from a self-evident law: the efficiency of labor power stands in inverse proportion to the length of time over which it is spent. The fewer hours a worker works, the more intensively he can work. Within certain limits, then, what is lost in duration is gained in the heightened tension of labor power. And the capitalist ensures, through the way he pays the worker, that the worker really does expend more labor power. In industries such as the potteries, where machinery plays little or no part, the introduction of the Factory Acts showed strikingly that merely shortening the working day increases to a remarkable degree the regularity, uniformity, order, continuity, and energy of labor. It seemed doubtful, however, whether the same effect would appear in the factory proper, where the worker's dependence on the continuous and uniform motion of the machinery had already imposed the strictest discipline. So when, in 1844, the reduction of the working day below twelve hours was being debated, the masters declared almost unanimously "that their overlookers in the different rooms took good care that the hands lost no time," that "the extent of vigilance and attention on the part of the workmen" was "hardly capable of being increased," and therefore that, with the speed of the machinery and other conditions unchanged, "to expect in a well managed factory any important result from increased attention of the workmen was an absurdity."

Experiments refuted this claim. Mr. Robert Gardner reduced the hours in his two large factories at Preston from twelve to eleven a day, beginning on 20 April 1844. After about a year, the result was that "the same amount of product for the same cost was received, and the workpeople as a whole earned in eleven hours as much wages as they did before in twelve." In the weaving department, where many kinds of patterned fancy goods were also woven, the conditions of work had not changed in the slightest. Yet the figures were these: "From 6th January to 20th April, 1844, with a twelve hours day, average weekly wages of each hand 10 shillings 1½ pence; from 20th April to 29th June, 1844, with a day of eleven hours, average weekly wages 10 shillings 3½ pence." More was produced in eleven hours than had been produced in twelve, and entirely because the workers applied themselves more steadily and wasted less time. They received the same wages and gained an hour of free time; the capitalist received the same output and saved an hour's worth of coal, gas, and similar expenses. Similar experiments, with similar success, were carried out in the mills of Messrs. Horrocks and Jackson.

The moral element played a significant part in these experiments. The workers told the factory inspector: "We work with more spirit, we have the reward ever before us of getting away sooner at night, and one active and cheerful spirit pervades the whole mill, from the youngest piecer to the oldest hand, and we can greatly help each other." The piecer was the child whose job was to join the broken threads.

Once the shorter day becomes compulsory, however, machinery becomes in capital's hands the objective means, systematically employed, for squeezing more labor out of a given span of time. This is done in two ways: by increasing the speed of the machines, and by giving each worker more machinery to tend.

Improvements in the steam engine raised the speed of the piston and at the same time, through greater economy of power, made it possible to drive more machinery from the same engine with the same consumption of coal, or even less. Improvements in the transmitting mechanism reduced friction and, in what most sharply distinguishes modern machinery from the old, cut the diameter and weight of the shafting to an ever-shrinking minimum. Finally, improvements in the working machines themselves either reduced their size while raising their speed and efficiency, as in the modern power loom; or enlarged their frames while also increasing the extent and number of their working parts, as in spinning mules; or quickened those working parts through barely noticeable changes of detail, such as those that, around 1855, raised the speed of the spindles on self-acting mules by one-fifth.

In England the reduction of the working day to twelve hours dates from 1832. In 1836 a manufacturer stated: "The labour now undergone in the factories is much greater than it used to be... compared with thirty or forty years ago... owing to the greater attention and activity required by the greatly increased speed which is given to the machinery." In 1844 Lord Ashley made the following statements in the House of Commons, backed by documentary evidence, in which a "stretch" is one full run of the mule's moving carriage, which the spinner had to follow across the floor, a "hank" is a skein of yarn of fixed length, and a "pick" is a single pass of the shuttle across the loom:

"The labour performed by those engaged in the processes of manufacture, is three times as great as in the beginning of such operations. Machinery has executed, no doubt, the work that would demand the sinews of millions of men; but it has also prodigiously multiplied the labour of those who are governed by its fearful movements.... In 1825, the labour of following a pair of mules spinning cotton yarn of No. 40 — reckoning 12 hours to the working-day — involved a necessity of walking 8 miles. In 1832, the distance travelled in following a pair of mules, spinning cotton yarn of the same number, was 20 miles, and frequently more. In 1825, the spinner put up daily, on each of these mules, 820 stretches, making a total of 1640 stretches in the course of the day. In 1832, the spinner put up on each mule 2200 stretches, making a total of 4400. In 1844, 2400 stretches, making a total of 4800; and in some cases the amount of labour required is even still greater.... I have another document sent to me in 1842, stating that the labour is progressively increasing — increasing not only because the distance to be travelled is greater, but because the quantity of goods produced is multiplied, while the hands are fewer in proportion than before; and, moreover, because an inferior species of cotton is now often spun, which it is more difficult to work.... In the carding-room there has also been a great increase of labour. One person there does the work formerly divided between two.... In the weaving-room, where a vast number of persons are employed, and principally females... the labour has increased within the last ten years fully 10 per cent, owing to the increased speed of the machinery. In 1838, the number of hanks spun per week was 18,000, in 1843 it amounted to 21,000. In 1819 the number of picks in power-loom-weaving per minute was 60 — in 1842 it was 140, showing a vast increase of labour."

Given the remarkable intensity of labor already reached by 1844 under the Twelve Hours' Act, the English manufacturers' claim at the time seemed justified: that no further progress in this direction was possible, and that every further reduction in hours must mean reduced output. But let us turn to the period that followed the introduction of the Ten Hours' Act in 1847 into the English cotton, woollen, silk, and flax mills.

"The speed of the spindles has increased upon throstles 500, and upon mules 1000 revolutions a minute; that is, the speed of the throstle spindle, which in 1839 was 4500 times a minute, is now" (1862) "5000; and of the mule spindle, that was 5000, is now 6000 times a minute." The throstle was a spinning frame that worked continuously, in contrast to the intermittent action of the mule. In 1852 James Nasmyth, the eminent civil engineer of Patricroft, near Manchester, wrote to the factory inspector Leonard Horner explaining the improvements made to the steam engine between 1848 and 1852. He continued: "I am confident that from the same weight of steam-engine machinery, we are now obtaining at least 50 per cent more duty or work performed on the average, and that in many cases the identical steam-engines which in the days of the restricted speed of 220 feet per minute, yielded 50 horse-power, are now yielding upwards of 100."

The factory inspectors' reports tell the same story: "The modern steam-engine of 100 horse-power is capable of being driven at a much greater force than formerly, arising from improvements in its construction, the capacity and construction of the boilers, &c.... Although the same number of hands are employed in proportion to the horse-power as at former periods, there are fewer hands employed in proportion to the machinery." Again: "The facts brought out by the return" (of 1856) "appear to be that the factory system is increasing rapidly; that although the same number of hands are employed in proportion to the horse-power as at former periods, there are fewer hands employed in proportion to the machinery; that the steam-engine is enabled to drive an increased weight of machinery by economy of force and other methods, and that an increased quantity of work can be turned off by improvements in machinery, and in methods of manufacture, by increase of speed of the machinery, and by a variety of other causes." And further: "The great improvements made in machines of every kind have raised their productive power very much. Without any doubt, the shortening of the hours of labour... gave the impulse to these improvements. The latter, combined with the more intense strain on the workman, have had the effect, that at least as much is produced in the shortened" (by two hours, or one-sixth) "working-day as was previously produced during the longer one."

However great the progress of English industry during the eight years from 1848 to 1856 under a ten-hour working day, it was far surpassed in the following six years, from 1856 to 1862.

In the silk factories, for instance, the figures were:

| Silk factories | Spindles | Looms | Operatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1856 | 1,093,799 | 9,260 | 56,131 |
| 1862 | 1,388,544 | 10,709 | 52,428 |

These figures show an increase of 26.9 percent in spindles and 15.6 percent in looms, alongside a decrease of 7 percent in the number of workers.

In the worsted mills, the spindles in use were:

| Worsted mills | Spindles | |
|---|---|---|
| 1850 | 875,830 | |
| 1856 | 1,324,549 | (increase 51.2%) |
| 1862 | 1,289,172 | (decrease 2.7%) |

If we subtract the doubling spindles, which twist two or more yarns together and are counted in the figures for 1856 but not in those for 1862, it turns out that after 1856 the number of spindles stayed nearly constant. On the other hand, after 1850 the speed of the spindles and looms was in many cases doubled. The worsted mills employed:

| Worsted mills | Power-looms | Operatives | Of whom children under 14 |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1850 | 32,617 | 79,737 | 9,956 |
| 1856 | 38,956 | 87,794 | 11,228 |
| 1862 | 43,048 | 86,063 | 13,178 |

Despite the much larger number of looms in 1862 compared with 1856, then, the total number of workers employed fell, while the number of children exploited rose.

On 27 April 1863, Mr. Ferrand told the House of Commons: "I have been informed by delegates from sixteen districts of Lancashire and Cheshire, on whose behalf I speak, that the work in the factories is constantly increasing as a result of improvements in machinery. Where formerly one person with two helpers tended two looms, one person now tends three looms without any helpers, and it is not unusual for one person to tend four. As the facts I have presented make clear, twelve hours' work is now compressed into less than ten. It is therefore self-evident how enormously the toil of the factory worker has increased over the last ten years."

A weaver working two modern power-looms now, in 1867, produces in a sixty-hour week twenty-six pieces of cloth of a given quality, length, and width, whereas on the old power-looms he could make no more than four such pieces. Soon after 1850, the cost of weaving one such piece had already fallen from two shillings and ninepence to fivepence-halfpenny.

The factory inspector Alexander Redgrave, writing in the *Journal of Arts* on 5 January 1872, observed: "Thirty years ago, in 1841, a spinner with three piecers was required to attend to no more than one pair of mules, carrying 300 to 324 spindles. Today, in 1871, he has to mind 2,200 spindles with the help of five piecers, and he produces no less than seven times as much yarn as in 1841." The mule was the spinning machine, and the piecers were the assistants who joined together broken threads as the machine ran.

The Factory Inspectors, then, praise the results of the Acts of 1844 and 1850 constantly and with good reason. Yet they also admit that the shortening of the working day has already provoked an intensification of labour severe enough to damage the worker's health and his capacity to work. One of them writes: "In most of the cotton, worsted, and silk mills, the exhausting state of excitement required for the workers to mind the machinery satisfactorily, machinery whose motion has been greatly accelerated within the last few years, seems to me not unlikely to be one of the causes of the excess mortality from lung disease that Dr. Greenhow has pointed out in his recent report on this subject." There cannot be the slightest doubt about where this leads. Once capital is permanently forbidden to lengthen the working day, it tends to compensate itself by systematically raising the intensity of labour and by turning every improvement in machinery into a more perfect means of exhausting the worker. That tendency must soon produce a situation in which a further reduction of working hours becomes inevitable once again.

### (D) Blunting of the Labourer's Intelligence. Increase of Accidents

he manipulates his tool, and that a hierarchy consequently arises, the result of the inequalities among the workers. We further saw that the difference between this primitive manufacture and modern industry lies precisely in the fact that the tool which shapes the raw material is taken out of the worker's hands and handed over to the machine. From then on, the machine, not the worker, shapes the raw material, and the worker has only to supervise the machine's activity. The capabilities of the tool are freed from the limits that are inseparable from human labour power. In manufacture, the tool can operate only as long as the worker who wields it is operating, and only with the intensity, skill, and strength that his own labour displays. In modern industry, one worker can easily replace another in supervising a machine, and the machine can keep running even while the worker sleeps or eats. This sweeps away the technical foundation on which the division of labour in manufacture rested. In place of the hierarchy of specialized workers that characterizes manufacture, the automatic factory shows a tendency to equalize every kind of work done by the machine-minders and reduce it all to one and the same level. In place of the artificially produced distinctions between detail workers, there appear the natural differences of age and sex.

Technically speaking, then, machinery throws the old division of labour overboard. Yet that division hangs on in the factory as a traditional habit inherited from manufacture, and capital afterwards systematically remoulds and re-establishes it in a more hideous form, as a means of exploiting labour power. The lifelong specialty of handling one and the same tool becomes the lifelong specialty of serving one and the same machine. Machinery is misused in order to turn the worker, from his earliest childhood, into a part of a detail-machine. This not only considerably lowers the cost of producing and maintaining him, but also makes his helpless dependence on the factory as a whole, and therefore on the capitalist, complete. Here, as everywhere else, we must distinguish between the greater productiveness that results from the development of the social process of production and the greater productiveness that results from the capitalist exploitation of that process.

In handicraft and manufacture, the worker makes use of a tool; in the factory, the machine makes use of him. In the first case, the movements of the instrument of labour originate with him; in the second, he must follow the movement of the machine. In manufacture, the workers are parts of a living mechanism. In the factory, there is a lifeless mechanism that exists independently of the worker, who becomes merely its living appendage. "The miserable routine of endless drudgery and toil, in which the same mechanical process is gone through over and over again, is like the labour of Sisyphus. The burden of labour, like the rock, keeps falling back on the worn-out labourer." Sisyphus, in the Greek myth, was condemned to roll a boulder up a hill for eternity, only to watch it roll back down each time he neared the top. Factory work exhausts the nervous system to the utmost, and at the same time it eliminates the varied play of the muscles and confiscates every particle of freedom, in both bodily and intellectual activity. Even the lightening of the labour becomes a kind of torture, because the machine does not free the worker from work; it strips the work of all interest. Modern industry, built on the foundation of machinery, finally completes the separation of the intellectual powers of production from manual labour, and the conversion of those powers into the dominion of capital over labour. The special skill of each individual, insignificant factory worker vanishes like an infinitesimal quantity beside the science, the gigantic physical forces, and the mass of labour embodied in the factory mechanism. Together with that mechanism, these constitute the power of the "master." In the master's mind, the machinery and his monopoly of it are inseparably fused, and so, whenever he quarrels with his "hands," he tells them with contempt: "The factory operatives should keep in wholesome memory the fact that theirs is really a low kind of skilled labour, and that there is none more easily acquired, none more amply paid for its quality, and none which even the least capable can pick up more quickly and thoroughly with a short training. . . . The master's machinery really plays a far more important part in production than the labour and skill of the operative, which six months' instruction can teach and an ordinary labourer can learn."

Two things give rise to a barrack discipline in the factory: the technical subordination of the worker to the uniform motion of the instruments of labour, and the peculiar makeup of the workforce, composed as it is of individuals of both sexes and all ages. In the factory this discipline is worked out into a complete system, and it brings to full development the work of supervision mentioned earlier, dividing the workforce into operatives and overseers, into the private soldiers and sergeants of an industrial army. "The main difficulty in the automatic factory . . . lay above all in training human beings to give up their irregular habits of work and to identify themselves with the unvarying regularity of the complex automaton." But the difficulty was overcome, and discipline was established. The overseer's book of penalties takes the place of the slave driver's lash. All punishments naturally come down to fines and deductions from wages, and the legislative talent of the factory Lycurgus, named after the legendary lawgiver of ancient Sparta, arranges matters so that breaking his laws is, where possible, more profitable to him than keeping them. As Engels writes: "The slavery in which the bourgeoisie holds the proletariat is nowhere more plainly exposed than in the factory system. Here all freedom ends, in law and in fact. The worker must be in the factory at half past five. If he arrives a few minutes late, he is punished; if he arrives ten minutes late, he is not allowed in until after breakfast, and so loses a quarter of a day's wages. He must eat, drink, and sleep at the word of command. . . . The despotic bell calls him from his bed and calls him from breakfast and dinner. And how does he fare inside the mill? There the master is the absolute lawgiver. He makes whatever regulations he likes; he alters his code and adds to it at will; and if he inserts the most utter nonsense, the courts tell the worker: since you entered into this contract voluntarily, you must now carry it out. . . . These workers are condemned to live under this mental and physical torture from their ninth year until their death."

We shall only touch here on the material conditions under which factory labour is carried out. Every sense organ is injured alike by the artificially raised temperature, the dust-laden air, and the deafening noise, to say nothing of the danger to life and limb among the densely packed machinery, which, with the regularity of the seasons, issues its list of the killed and wounded in the industrial battle. The factory system brings economy in the social means of production to an early, hothouse ripeness. In the hands of capital, this economy is turned into the systematic robbery of what the worker needs to live while he is at work: robbery of space, light, and air, and of protection for his body against the dangerous and unhealthy conditions that accompany the productive process, not to mention robbery of any arrangements for the worker's comfort.

The protection the Factory Acts provide against dangerous machinery has had a beneficial effect. But, as the Reports of the Inspectors of Factories for 31 October 1866 state, "there are other sources of accident that did not exist twenty years ago, above all the increased speed of the machinery. Wheels, rollers, spindles, and shuttles are now driven at higher and ever-increasing speeds. Fingers must be quicker and more nimble in picking up a broken thread, because if they are placed hesitantly or carelessly, they are sacrificed. . . . A large number of accidents are caused by the workers' eagerness to get their work done quickly. It must be remembered that it is of the highest importance to manufacturers that their machinery should run without interruption, producing yarn and goods. Every minute of stoppage is a loss not only of power but of production. The overseers, who have an interest in the quantity of work turned out, urge the workers to keep the machinery running, and it matters just as much to those operatives who are paid by weight or by the piece that the machines should be kept going. As a result, although cleaning machinery while it is in motion is strictly forbidden in many factories, indeed in most, it is nevertheless the constant practice in most if not all of them. . . . From this cause alone, 906 accidents have occurred in the last six months. . . . Although a great deal of cleaning goes on every day, Saturday is generally the day set aside for cleaning the machinery thoroughly, and much of this is done while the machinery is running. . . . Since cleaning is not paid for, the workers try to finish it as quickly as possible. Hence the number of accidents on Fridays, and especially on Saturdays, is much larger than on any other day. On Friday the excess is nearly 12 per cent over the average of the first four days of the week; on Saturday it is 25 per cent over the average of the preceding five days. If the working hours on Saturday are taken into account, seven and a half hours compared with ten and a half on other days, the excess on Saturday rises to 65 per cent over the average of the other five days."

I will also quote the following from Leonard Horner's official report of 31 October 1855: "I have heard some mill owners speak with inexcusable flippancy about certain accidents, describing the loss of a finger, for instance, as a trifling matter. A working man's livelihood and prospects depend so heavily on his fingers that losing any of them is a very serious matter for him. Whenever I have heard such thoughtless remarks, I have usually put this question: suppose you needed another worker, and two men applied, equally well qualified in every other respect, except that one had lost a thumb or a forefinger. Which would you hire? There was never any hesitation about the answer." . . .

It must nevertheless be noted that in the factories that have been subject to the Factory Acts the longest, with their compulsory limits on working hours and their other regulations, many of the older abuses have disappeared. The very improvement of machinery requires, to a certain extent, an improvement in the construction of factory buildings, and this benefits the workers, as the factory inspectors' report of 31 October 1863 notes.

### (E) Starvation of the Wage-Earners

The contest between the capitalist and the wage-labourer dates back to the very origin of capital. It raged on throughout the entire period of manufacture. But the writers of that period treat the division of labour chiefly as a means of making up for a potential shortage of workers, not as a means of actually displacing those already employed. If we say that it would take 100 million people in England to spin, with the old spinning wheel, the cotton that 500,000 people now spin with mules, this does not mean that the mules took the place of those millions, who never existed. If, on the other hand, we say that in England the power-loom threw 800,000 weavers onto the streets, we are referring to weavers who existed and were actually replaced or displaced by the looms. During the manufacturing period, handicraft labour, however altered by the division of labour, remained the foundation. The demands of the new colonial markets could not be met by the relatively small number of town workers inherited from the Middle Ages, and manufacture proper opened new fields of production to the rural population that the breakup of the feudal system had driven off the land. In that period, therefore, the division of labour and cooperation in the workshop were seen in a positive light: they made workers more productive. But once the instrument of labour takes the form of a machine, it immediately becomes a competitor of the worker himself. From then on, the self-expansion of capital through machinery is directly proportional to the number of workers whose livelihoods that machinery has destroyed. As soon as the handling of the tool becomes the work of a machine, the exchange-value of labour power vanishes along with its use-value. The worker becomes unsaleable, like paper money that has been withdrawn from circulation and is no longer accepted in payment. The part of the working class that machinery has thus made superfluous, that is, no longer immediately necessary for the self-expansion of capital, either goes under in the unequal contest between the old handicrafts and manufactures and the machines, or else floods all the more easily accessible branches of industry, swamps the labour market, and drives the price of labour power below its value. The workers are offered two great consolations: first, that their sufferings are only temporary; and second, that machinery takes over a given field of production only by degrees, so that the extent and intensity of its destructive effect are reduced. The first consolation cancels out the second. When machinery takes over an industry gradually, it produces chronic misery among the workers who compete with it. Where the transition is rapid, the effect is acute and is felt by great masses of people. History reveals no tragedy more horrible than the gradual extinction of the English hand-loom weavers, an extinction that stretched over several decades and was finally sealed in 1838. Many of them died of starvation, and many, with their families, barely subsisted for a long time on twopence-halfpenny a day. English cotton machinery, on the other hand, had an acute effect in India. The Governor-General reported in 1834–35: "The misery hardly finds a parallel in the history of commerce. The bones of the cotton weavers are bleaching on the plains of India."

Even within modern industry, the continual improvement of machinery has a similar effect. "The object of improved machinery is to diminish manual labour." "The application of power to machinery previously driven by hand happens almost daily. . . . Minor improvements in machinery, aimed at economizing power, producing better work, turning out more work in the same time, or replacing a child, a woman, or a man, are constant; and though they sometimes seem of little consequence, they have fairly important results." "Wherever a process requires particular dexterity and steadiness of hand, it is taken away as soon as possible from the skilled worker, who is prone to irregularities of many kinds, and placed in the charge of a special mechanism so self-regulating that a child can supervise it." In 1860, the peak year of the English cotton industry, who would have imagined the galloping improvements in machinery, and the corresponding displacement of workers, that the stimulus of the American Civil War called into being over the following three years? A couple of examples from the Reports of the Inspectors of Factories will be enough. A Manchester manufacturer states: "We used to have 75 carding engines; now we have 12, doing the same amount of work, of equal if not better quality. . . . We are saving £10 a week in wages, and we estimate our saving in waste at about 10 per cent of the cotton consumed." Carding engines were the machines that combed and straightened raw cotton fibres before spinning. An inspector reports: "In another fine-spinning mill in Manchester, I was told that through increased speed and the adoption of certain self-acting processes, the number of workers had been reduced by a quarter in one department and by more than half in another, and that the introduction of the combing machine in place of the second carding had considerably reduced the number of hands formerly employed in the carding room." Another spinning mill is estimated to save 10 per cent in labour. Messrs. Gilmore, spinners in Manchester, state: "In our blowing-room department," where the raw cotton was opened and cleaned, "we reckon that with the new machinery our expense is fully one-third less in wages and in hands. . . . In two other departments it is about one-third less in expense, and likewise one-third less in hands; in the spinning room, about one-third less in expense. But that is not all. When our yarn goes to the manufacturers, it is so much improved by our new machinery that they can produce more cloth, and more cheaply, than from yarn produced by the old machinery."

The following table shows the overall result of the mechanical improvements in the English cotton industry brought about by the American Civil War.

| Year | Number of Factories | Power-Looms | Spindles | Persons Employed |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1858 | 2,210 | 298,847 | 28,010,217 | 379,213 |
| 1861 | 2,887 | 399,992 | 30,387,494 | 451,569 |
| 1868 | 2,549 | 379,329 | 32,000,014 | 401,064 |

Between 1861 and 1868, then, 338 cotton factories disappeared; in other words, more productive machinery on a larger scale became concentrated in the hands of fewer capitalists. The number of power-looms fell by 20,663, but since their output rose over the same period, an improved loom must have produced more than an old one. Finally, the number of spindles rose by 1,612,541, while the number of workers fell by 50,505. The "temporary" misery that the cotton crisis inflicted on the workers was deepened, and turned from temporary into permanent, by the rapid and persistent advance of machinery.

But machinery does not act only as a competitor that defeats the worker and is always on the verge of making him superfluous. It is also the most powerful weapon for suppressing strikes, those periodic revolts of the working class against the autocracy of capital. According to Gaskell, the steam engine was from the very beginning an antagonist of human power, one that allowed the capitalist to crush the growing demands of the workers, which threatened the newly born factory system with crisis. One could write an entire history of the inventions made since 1830 for the sole purpose of supplying capital with weapons against working-class revolt.
