**Summary of Department I's Variable Capital Flow:**
The money paid as wages by Department I capitalists doesn't come back to them directly from their own workers (who buy mc, not mp). It goes first to Department II capitalists (when workers buy mc). Only when Department II capitalists use that money to buy mp from Department I does the wage-money return (indirectly) to Department I capitalists.

### The Key Condition for Simple Reproduction

For simple reproduction to occur smoothly (where the economy just replaces itself without growing or shrinking), a fundamental condition must be met:
* The part of Department I's annual product that represents its workers' wages (v) plus its capitalists' surplus-value (s) must be equal in value to the constant capital (c) used up in Department II.
* This can be written as: **I (v + s) = II c**

Using our example: I (1000v + 1000s) = 2000. This must equal II c, which is 2000c. So, the condition holds.

### Consumption Within Department II

Now let's look at the wages (v) and surplus-value (s) components of Department II's product.
* Workers in Department II receive wages from their capitalist employers. They use this money to buy back a part of what they themselves produced (means of consumption).
* This way, the capitalists in Department II get back, in money form, the capital they advanced as wages. (It's as if they paid their workers in tokens that could only be spent on the company's own goods.)

Department II, which produces means of consumption, can be thought of in two main subdivisions:

* **A) Necessary Means of Consumption:** These are goods needed by workers (food, basic clothing, housing). They also form part of what capitalists consume. For our purposes, we can group all items habitually consumed by workers here, whether strictly "physiologically necessary" or not (like tobacco, if it's a common item of consumption).
* **B) Luxury Means of Consumption:** These are goods consumed exclusively by capitalists. They can, therefore, only be exchanged for surplus-value (profits), not for workers' wages.

**Money Flow for Necessities vs. Luxuries:**
* **Necessary Goods (IIA):** When capitalists in subdivision IIA (producing necessities) pay wages, this money returns to them directly when their workers buy these essential goods. The workers' spending provides the means for this money to circulate back.
* **Luxury Goods (IIB):** It's different for subdivision IIB (producing luxuries). Workers do not buy these luxury items. So, if the wages paid to workers making luxuries are to return to the IIB capitalists in money form, it can't happen directly. An intermediate step or exchange is needed (for example, IIB capitalists sell luxuries to other capitalists, who might have received their money from selling necessities to workers, or from Department I capitalists spending their surplus-value).
A balanced proportion is needed between the production of necessary goods and luxury goods for the system to work smoothly, similar to the main condition between Department I and II.

Assuming simple reproduction, we come necessarily to the following result:
1.

Assuming simple reproduction, we come necessarily to the following result:

1.  The newly created value in Department I (which is the sum of wages and surplus-value, I(v+s), and exists in the physical form of **means of production**) must be equal in value to the constant capital used up in Department II (IIc).
    * If the value of I(v+s) were smaller than the value of IIc, then Department II (which makes means of consumption) could not buy enough means of production to fully replace what it used up. Therefore, Department II could not continue producing at the same level as before.
    * If, on the other hand, the value of I(v+s) were larger than the value of IIc, then there would be a surplus of means of production from Department I that would not be used.

2.  The total wages paid to workers who are engaged in producing luxury goods (Department IIB) must be less than the total surplus-value (profit) made by those capitalists who produce necessary means of consumption (Department IIA).
    * This means that the funds available from the profits of necessity-good producers (which are partly spent on luxuries) must be enough to cover the wages of workers in the luxury sector. If the wages in the luxury sector were greater, it would imply an imbalance, as the primary consumers of luxuries are capitalists (using their surplus-value).

## Supplement: The Essence of Marx's Theory of Crises (by Julian Borchardt)

Because bourgeois and socialist economic thought diverge so fundamentally, their views on economic crises differ on almost every point. Both sides agree, however, that a crisis is a grave disturbance of the equilibrium between production and consumption. As Paul Mombert writes: "A state of affairs in which supply and demand balance each other in the commodity market, so that a complete equilibrium exists between production and consumption, and in which the goods produced find buyers as easily as the demand for goods can be satisfied, appears to be the economic ideal." Today the link between producer and consumer runs through so many intermediaries, often complicated ones, that a fundamental truth is easily overlooked: production exists for the sake of consumption, and goods are made to satisfy a need for them. It follows naturally from this truth that an equilibrium must be possible. As much of each commodity should be produced as consumers need, neither more nor less. Where this fails, too many goods or too few will be produced, or else goods other than those wanted, and the market will be disturbed in proportion to the size of the discrepancy. No special training in political economy is needed to see that in times of crisis an immense stock of unsalable goods lies piled up, while at the same moment the great mass of consumers suffers a scarcity of goods. One cannot simply conclude that the gap between production and consumption is the fault of either producers or consumers. The goods produced may well correspond, in quantity and in kind, to what consumers need. Yet the highly complicated apparatus that now carries goods from producers to consumers can break down, so that goods sit unsold that are urgently needed elsewhere. Whatever its causes, then, a crisis certainly consists in a disturbance of the equilibrium between production and consumption.

The question arises whether this was always so. Was there a time when no such disturbance occurred, and when it may even have been impossible? No precise answer can be given, because we know far less about the economic life of primitive peoples than certain vivid descriptions might lead us to believe. It is reasonable to assume, however, that small bands of hunters and gatherers, who seek only to satisfy their immediate wants, would find it hard to produce either more or less than those wants required. Turning to the ancient Germanic tribes of the age of Augustus and Hermann, the chieftain the Romans called Arminius, we find the historian Steinhausen writing of them in his study of early Germanic culture: "As with all peoples living in a state of nature, labor knows only one motive: urgent need arising from scarcity. Regular labor does not exist. The activity that results from the search for food, from the necessities of shelter, and from the satisfaction of other elementary wants is at first regarded as labor only to a limited degree.... Each household procures and produces for itself everything it needs." Picture such a primitive Germanic tribe, perhaps only a few dozen people, roaming the forest, hunting, gathering roots and fruit, and raiding other tribes, and it becomes clear at once that the notion of these people "producing" more or less than they immediately needed cannot be sustained.

The notion is hard to entertain even at much higher stages of civilization, so long as "self-production," meaning production for one's own needs, remains the predominant form. This kind of production did not always keep the primitive features just described, for economic activity became regulated. But take a tribe of a few hundred or even a few thousand members that regularly raises cattle and tills the soil, in addition to hunting and making war. As long as "each household procures and produces for itself everything it needs," the needs of every individual are well known, and all productive activity will obviously be directed solely toward satisfying those known needs. The same holds for the communal production of such small tribes. Excess production, or "overproduction," could of course arise from an unusually good harvest or an unusually rich haul of plunder from a raid. Even then, disposing of the surplus should have caused no noticeable difficulty. We may therefore assume that throughout all the centuries in which self-production prevailed, the equilibrium between production and consumption did in fact exist, since production had to be based exclusively on the needs of the consumers.

However long it may have lasted, the period of self-production nonetheless came to an end. The steady growth of population, and the corresponding growth of its requirements, led to the division of labor and to the production of commodities, that is, goods made for sale. Consider the earlier or later Middle Ages, when town dwellers lived mainly, if not entirely, by their crafts. These medieval townspeople were all peasants as well. Inside or outside the city walls they owned meadows on which they grazed their cattle. But they also practiced their respective crafts, which supplied an ever larger share of their livelihood. When a shoemaker made shoes continually, a tailor clothes, a weaver cloth, and so on, it was perfectly plain that none of them was working to satisfy his own requirements, but rather those of others. The finished products had to be sold, and were intended for sale from the very start. Commodities were being produced.

With this arises the possibility of a rupture in the equilibrium between production and consumption, because the direct connection between the two is severed. It should be noted that the sale of one's own products, at least among the Germans, did not originate directly in the needs of consumers but in the growth of the volume of production. (Foreign products, by contrast, had been imported and sold by foreign traders since the earliest times.) The great landed estates that arose under the Frankish dynasty, roughly between 500 and 900, gathered considerable numbers of people onto a single vast property under the command of a single lord. For their systematic cultivation these estates called into being a labor organization: a highly differentiated body of officials, warriors, administrators, peasants, and craftsmen. Here, then, is where handicraft originated, and only here could it have done so. On a small peasant holding, where perhaps fewer than a dozen people lived together, it would have occurred to no one to devote himself exclusively to, say, making clothes for so few people, for he would not have had enough work to fill his time. On the large estates, however, where hundreds of people had to be supplied with food, clothing, and other necessities, labor was split up from the outset so that one man made only clothes, another only tools and utensils, and so on. It is precisely to this division of labor that the steadily rising productivity must be attributed. Production grew continually until it finally exceeded the needs of lord and dependents alike. The sale of this surplus began, and German history shows how the development of trade gradually detached the craftsman from the estate, led him to settle in market centers, and so brought about the founding and expansion of towns.

Even so, we know of no commercial crises during the Middle Ages, no serious ruptures, that is, of the equilibrium between consumption and production. Or at least we know only of crises that arose from external causes, war above all, and that resulted from production falling short of what consumers demanded. We read of none that arose, as crises do today, from internal causes and from "overproduction." This is entirely understandable. The early medieval craftsman really worked only for his immediate neighborhood. He knew his neighbors' wants precisely in advance and could regulate his output accordingly. The shoemaker, for instance, at first made boots only to order, or only such boots as he was certain of selling at once. Then came the merchant and craft guilds, which divided the market precisely among their members. These primitive conditions did not last. Commerce and trade developed, not only between towns but between countries, and with every such extension the possibility of a rupture in the equilibrium grew. The requirements of a distant town, and especially of a foreign country, could not be foreseen as accurately as those of one's own neighborhood, and so production could not be adjusted to them with the same precision. Nonetheless, the connections between production and consumption remained clear, simple, and visible at a glance. As already noted, we know of no serious disturbances.

We may therefore regard it as established that during the period of self-production the equilibrium between production and consumption was, so to speak, self-evident, because production was determined by the needs of the consumers. Those needs then gave rise to the division of labor, and so created the possibility of a rupture in the equilibrium. This disturbing factor was nonetheless necessary, since it alone could generate the forces capable of satisfying the increased requirements.

From the simple production of commodities, the course of development leads to the dawn of the capitalist era. Where does the difference between capitalism and simple commodity production lie? Seen from outside, it lies in the producer's loss of independence. The craftsman is his own master and works on his own account, while the wage laborer is employed by the capitalist.

Seen from inside, a more essential difference appears: under capitalism the organization of labor is more complicated. When the medieval craftsman is assisted by journeymen and apprentices, he is obliged to teach them the craft, and each of them must learn every part of it. The capitalist, by contrast, gathers a number of laborers in his workshop from the outset for the purpose of producing as many goods as possible. The training of any individual worker interests him only insofar as it enables the whole workforce to produce more. It soon becomes evident that this purpose is best served not by giving each worker a broad and varied training, but by assigning him one definite partial operation to which he must thoroughly accustom himself. Then, through the systematic cooperation of all, output increases. This is how manufacture arises.

This systematic cooperation, however, introduces a new factor into the process of production, one that was previously absent. The quantity of goods to be turned out is no longer determined solely by the requirements of the consumers; it now depends also on the necessities of production itself. Marx gives an example from a type foundry of earlier times, where printers' type was made in three stages. One founder could cast 2,000 pieces of type an hour, a breaker could break off the excess metal from 4,000, and a rubber could polish 8,000. Consequently a working group of one rubber, two breakers, and four founders had to operate together. This cooperation, this mutual dependence, also requires that 8,000 pieces of type be produced every hour, and no fewer, since otherwise some of the workers would not be fully employed. Suppose that only 6,000 were needed. One founder would then have to be dismissed, but the rubber and the two breakers would have to be kept on, even though they would inevitably stand idle part of the time and so cause the capitalist a loss. The result is that the capitalist must find a market for 8,000 pieces of type an hour. Otherwise he cannot make full use of his productive apparatus, which costs him money and cannot be scaled down.

We see, then, how the connection between production and consumption is progressively dissolved. Even in the early days of the capitalist era now under discussion, capitalists find themselves compelled to increase their output without any regard for the wants of consumers. The aim of production now lies, so to speak, within production itself. Originally, of course, the growth of production was caused by the growing requirements of consumers, and the new mechanism of production was created in order to satisfy that rising demand. But once it exists, the new mechanism leads an independent life and must operate with complete disregard for whether its activity merely meets the requirements of consumers or exceeds them.

Thus, for the first time, overproduction became possible. "Overproduction" is to be understood here in the rational sense of the word, as production beyond the requirements of the consumers. The connection between producer and consumer no longer exists, and the equilibrium fluctuates. But, to repeat, this development was absolutely necessary in order to generate the forces capable of satisfying the increased requirements.

The tendency for production and consumption to fall out of balance, a rupture produced, as we have seen, by the separate necessities of each, is plainly visible in modern capitalist society, and there it is carried to its furthest consequences. Under these conditions, equilibrium is out of the question. On one side, the productive apparatus has grown enormously and turns out immense quantities of commodities, which makes it far less able than even the old manufacturing system to adjust itself to what consumers actually need. Suppose, for instance, that the demand for steel rises beyond what the existing means of production can supply. To meet that additional demand, no one can simply build a small steelworks, because a steelworks pays only if it is large. Yet a large steelworks immediately produces a surplus far greater than the increase in demand that called it into being. On the other side, the working class under capitalism receives only a portion of the value it produces. The gap between what workers are able to consume and what they would have to consume for every commodity produced to find a buyer therefore keeps widening, because the process of development continually expands production. Finally, as production grows, the process becomes not only larger but more complicated, and therefore far more vulnerable to disturbance. To show why this is so, we must once again go into some detail.

When primitive man, living in a virgin forest, feels a want of any kind, say hunger, he goes out to hunt, or he gathers roots and fruit, and he satisfies his hunger with whatever he kills or finds. Today, a whole series of intermediate factors stands between a man's hunger and its satisfaction. To produce the bread on our table, the baker had to work. For that work he needs an oven, the equipment that goes with it, and the building that houses them both. He buys flour from the miller, who grinds the grain in his mill. Ovens, mills, and their machinery cannot be built without factories, and those factories in turn obtain iron, timber, and coal, in a more or less processed form, from large enterprises such as mines. The needs of modern civilized humanity are thus satisfied not directly but by a very roundabout route. Delivering bread to the consumer, and the same holds for every article of consumption, is only the final link in a long chain made up chiefly of deliveries of means of production from one producer to another. These detours were necessary to raise the abundance of production to its present height. If production and consumption are not to fall out of balance, then the baker must supply exactly as much bread as consumers need, the factories must supply precisely the number of ovens required for baking, and the mines must supply precisely the right amount of coal and iron. An exact equilibrium, in short, must hold among all the various branches of production. This is impossible, for the reason already given: in order to develop the productive forces, the process of production must obey its own laws, which arise from its own organization, and so it cannot accommodate itself to what consumers require. Marx showed how strictly exact the balance between the different branches must be in the celebrated formulas of the second volume of *Capital*, which Hilferding summarizes well in his *Finance Capital*. A single example will briefly illustrate what the problem means.

For the sake of simplicity, assume that production as a whole simply continues on the same scale as before, without expanding. In that case, the capitalists must possess the means of production and the means of subsistence they need not in the form of money but in kind. Workers cannot eat money, and money cannot weave yarn or smelt iron. The total available stock of means of subsistence and means of production must therefore be distributed among the different branches in such a way that each branch can go on producing. The slightest imbalance anywhere must produce a disturbance. In what proportions, then, must the distribution be made?

Call the means of consumption "me" and the means of production "mp." Suppose that at the end of the year the capitalists who produce means of consumption hold 3,000 units of me in kind. Over the coming year they must feed their workers and themselves out of this stock, and enough must be left over to exchange for the means of production they need. Let us assume that they require 500 for their workers and 500 for themselves, and that they use the remaining 2,000 units of me to buy mp.

Through this last transaction, the capitalists who produce means of production come into possession of 2,000 units of me in kind, which they can use over the following year to feed their workers and themselves. If the proportion is the same as in the consumption-goods group, they will give 1,000 to their workers and keep 1,000 for themselves. Now, if the capitalists in the production-goods group are to keep producing, they must have enough mp left from their previous output to employ the number of workers whom 1,000 units of me will feed for a year. Assuming the same proportions, that quantity of mp is 4,000. Thus, if production in the consumption-goods group requires 2,000 units of means of production, plus 500 in wages, plus 500 in surplus value for the capitalists, then, for equilibrium to hold, the production-goods group must have at its disposal 4,000 units of means of production, plus 1,000 in wages, plus 1,000 in surplus value. This is the meaning of Marx's celebrated formula:

I. Means of production: 4,000c + 1,000v + 1,000s = 6,000

II. Means of consumption: 2,000c + 500v + 500s = 3,000

Here *s* stands for surplus value, *v* for variable capital, that is, wages, and *c* for constant capital, that is, the means of production. One glance at this formula is enough to show that, amid the complexities of capitalist production, so delicate an equilibrium cannot possibly be achieved. And so far we have treated the matter only in the broadest outline. We have put all the capitalists who produce means of production into a single group, and all those who produce means of consumption into another. Clearly, however, the equilibrium must also hold across far more intricate subdivisions within these groups. The capitalists who manufacture baking ovens, for instance, must have at their disposal exactly the quantity of means of consumption and means of production suited to their branch, as determined by the needs of the bakeries. Moreover, we have assumed that production continues on the same scale without expanding, which never happens in reality, and the constant expansion of the process makes the conditions of equilibrium more delicate and more complicated still. Nor have we considered the different categories of means of production, namely what is called fixed and circulating capital, which complicate the conditions of equilibrium yet again. And finally, we have left aside the fact that every exchange, whether of means of production for means of consumption, of means of production for one another, of means of consumption for one another, or of labor for food, takes place through the medium of money, and that the use of money brings new disturbing elements into being.

It is therefore certain that capitalist society cannot achieve even an approximate equilibrium between production and consumption, and that crises are consequently inevitable. At the same time, we see how necessary these disturbances are for bringing about the development of the productive forces, which alone can satisfy the constantly growing needs of consumers. The only question that remains is whether these antagonisms can in time be resolved and reconciled in a higher synthesis, and if so, how that can be accomplished.

Engels gave the answer with classical clarity in his pamphlet *Principles of Communism*, published after his death. The immense development of the productive forces that we owe to capitalism was also the cause of the complete rupture between production and consumption, a rupture that at first sight seemed beyond repair. Crises are the inevitable result of the fact that the productive forces, in order to develop, can take no account of the needs either of consumers or of other branches of production. Production must go on, whether or not a market exists, so that the value of the vast productive apparatus does not depreciate. Under these conditions, periodic catastrophes cannot be avoided. Yet the enlarged forces of production also create ever more colossal quantities of commodities, and they make it possible to produce vaster quantities still in the future. The whole meaning of the economic problem has thereby been changed, indeed inverted. In past ages, the problem was how production could satisfy the needs of consumers. Today, on the contrary, it is how the immense quantities of commodities that can now be produced with ease can be made accessible to consumers so that they are actually consumed. This is the great problem to be solved in a future that is no longer distant, for there is reason to fear that the economic structure of modern society cannot long withstand the immense upheavals to which it is constantly exposed. Once we are convinced that the problem cannot and will not be solved along the only lines on which a solution has so far been sought, namely by restricting production, and that it can be solved only by increasing consumption, so that everything produced now and in the future is actually consumed, then boundless and joyful prospects open before us. We can then foresee the arrival of social conditions in which everyone will be freed from the burden of material hardship and distress, and in which humanity, its economic existence secured, will be able to devote itself to new and higher tasks. In this society of the future, personal freedom and the well-being of all, without exception, will for the first time in history become realities, and each individual will at the same time be able to develop his personal aptitudes and capacities to the full.
